\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(X)}\right.}{q(X)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(X)}\right]}=\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(X_{\sigma})}\right.}{f(X_{\sigma})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(X_{\sigma})}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(X)}\right.}{f(X)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(X)}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(X)}\right.}{f(X)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(X)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}\,.

(Using Lemma
1
, Eq. (
1
))

(2)

Therefore,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has the same probabilities of outputting
accept
and
reject
as
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and thus inherits the accuracy guarantees of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Next, we show replicability of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Similar to Lemma
1
, we have:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}&=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right]}\\
&=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}\,,\end{split}

(3)

where in the last line, we use the structure of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Using the definition of
q
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right.}{\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right]}

(Via triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right]}\,.

(4)

Recall that the distribution of sample sets remains identical after permutation, so
X_{\sigma}\overset{d}{=}X
and
X^{\prime}_{\sigma}\overset{d}{=}X^{\prime}
. Hence, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{f(X_{\sigma})\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime}_{\sigma})}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{f(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ f(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r\sim\Unif[0,1]}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{1}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}\leq\rho

(Using Lemma
1
)

Hence, the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 3

(Label Invariant Algorithm)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)
be a
\rho
-replicable algorithm for testing a symmetric property
\mathcal{P}
of discrete distributions over
[n]
, using
s
i.i.d. samples
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{s})
drawn from an underlying distribution
p
, and randomness
r
. The algorithm outputs a binary decision in
\{\text{{accept} },\text{{reject} }\}
. The accuracy of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is determined by two parameters
\epsilon
and
\delta
in
(0,1)
, satisfying the following:

•

If
p\in\mathcal{P}
, then

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}\geq 1-\delta\,.

•

If
p
is
\epsilon
-far from
\mathcal{P}
, then

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{reject} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{reject} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{reject} }}\right]}\geq 1-\delta\,.

Then, there exists another
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)
that solves the same problem using
s
samples with the same accuracy, and is invariant to the labels of the samples. That is, for every seed
r
, permutation function
\pi:[s]\rightarrow[s]
, and sample set
X
, we have:

\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\mathcal{A}_{3}(\pi(X);r)\,,

where
\pi(X)
denotes
\left(\pi(X_{1}),\pi(X_{2}),\ldots,\pi(X_{s})\right)
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is invariant to the order of the samples, and operates in the canonical format of comparing a deterministic function to a random threshold, as defined in Lemma
1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)
be the algorithm defined in Lemma
2
corresponding to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, with an associated deterministic function
q:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow[0,1]
. Define a new deterministic function
h:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow[0,1]
as:

h(X)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}q(\pi(X))\,.

That is,
h
computes the average of
q
over all permutations of the sample labels.

The algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)
behaves similarly to
\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)
: for
r\sim\Unif([0,1])
, it outputs
accept
if
r\leq h(X)
, and
reject
otherwise. Since
q
and therefore
h
are invariant to the sample order,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is order-invariant.

Next, we verify the accuracy guarantee of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
. Consider any permutation function
\pi:[n]\rightarrow[n]
. For any sample set
X
, the permuted sample
\pi(X)
can be viewed as drawn i.i.d. from
p^{\pi}\coloneqq p\circ\pi^{-1}
. That is, for all
j
, the probability of element
\pi(j)
under
p^{\pi}
equals
p(j)
. We now follow the structure of the proof in Lemma
2
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(X)}\right.}{h(X)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(X)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\pi(X))}\right.}{q(\pi(X))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\pi(X))}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(X)}\right.}{q(X)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(X)}\right]}

(since
\pi(X)\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}
when
X\sim p^{\otimes s}
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

(by Lemma
2
, Eq. (
2
))

The above shows that the probability
\mathcal{A}_{3}
accepts under
p
is the average acceptance probability of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
under
p^{\pi}
for all permutations
\pi
.

Now, for symmetric properties: if
p\in\mathcal{P}
, then by definition of symmetry (Definition
8
), each
p^{\pi}
also belongs to
\mathcal{P}
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
accepts each
p^{\pi}
with probability at least
1-\delta
, and therefore
\mathcal{A}_{3}
accepts with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Conversely, if
p
is
\epsilon
-far from
\mathcal{P}
, then so is every
p^{\pi}
, since total variation distance is invariant under permutation. In particular, for every
\pi
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(p,\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=\min_{d\in\mathcal{P}}\mathrm{dist}(p,d)=\min_{d\in\mathcal{P}}\mathrm{dist}(p^{\pi},d^{\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{d\in\mathcal{P}}\mathrm{dist}(p^{\pi},d)=\mathrm{dist}(p^{\pi},\mathcal{P})\,,

where the inequality holds since
d^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}
for symmetric
\mathcal{P}
. By applying the same reasoning in the reverse direction using
\pi^{-1}
, we conclude:

\mathrm{dist}(p,\mathcal{P})=\mathrm{dist}(p^{\pi},\mathcal{P})\,.

Hence, each
p^{\pi}
is also
\epsilon
-far from
\mathcal{P}
, so
\mathcal{A}_{0}
rejects each with probability at least
1-\delta
. Therefore,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
accepts with probability less than
\delta
, as desired.

We now prove the replicability of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
. Using the same logic as in Equations (
17
) and (
4
), we get:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}&\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(\pi(X))-q(\pi(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(\pi(X))-q(\pi(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(\pi(X))-q(\pi(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}\,.\end{split}

Since
\pi(X)
, when
X\sim p^{\otimes s}
, is distributed as
(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}
, we have:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}&\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}\,.\end{split}

Using Equation (
17
), we get:

\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(X)-q(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}\leq\rho\,.

The inequality holds because
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
\rho
-replicable for any
p^{\pi}
(by Lemma
2
). Therefore:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}&\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}\leq\rho\,.\end{split}

Hence, the proof is complete.∎

Lemma 4

(Permutation-Robust Replicability)
.

Algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}
, introduced in Lemma
3
, satisfies a stronger stability assumption—namely,
\rho
-permutation robust replicability. That is, for every distribution
p
over
[n]
and every permutation
\pi:[n]\rightarrow[n]
, we have:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{{r\sim\Unif[0,1]},X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}\leq\rho\,,

where
p^{\pi}\coloneqq p\circ\pi^{-1}
.

Proof.

Similar to Equation (
17
), and given the structure of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
as a random threshold algorithm, we have:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right]}\\
&\quad\quad=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{h(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ h(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right.}{\left|{h(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ h(X^{\prime})}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{h(X)\penalty\ -\penalty\ h(X^{\prime})}\right|}\right]}\\
&\quad\quad=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}}q(\tau^{\prime}(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}}q(\tau^{\prime}(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}}q(\tau^{\prime}(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}\,.\end{split}

The last line above follows from the definition of
h
. Here,
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are permutation functions from
[n]
to
[n]
.

Now, we pair up
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
as follows: fix a permutation
\tau
, and define
\tau^{\prime}:[n]\rightarrow[n]
by

\tau^{\prime}(i)\coloneqq\tau(\pi^{-1}(i))\,\quad\quad\text{for all }i\in[n]\,.

Note that since both
\tau
and
\pi
are bijections, so is
\tau^{\prime}
. Thus,
\tau^{\prime}
is a permutation function. Moreover, the mapping from
\tau
to
\tau^{\prime}
is itself a bijection. To see this, suppose for contradiction that two distinct permutations
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
map to the same
\tau^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\tau^{\prime}_{1}=\tau^{\prime}_{2}
. Then for every
i\in[n]
,

\tau^{\prime}_{1}(\pi(i))=\tau^{\prime}_{2}(\pi(i))\,,

which implies

\tau_{1}(\pi^{-1}(\pi(i)))=\tau_{2}(\pi^{-1}(\pi(i)))\quad\Rightarrow\quad\tau_{1}(i)=\tau_{2}(i)\,.

Thus,
\tau_{1}
must be identical to
\tau_{2}
, contradicting our assumption. Therefore, the mapping is injective. Since each
\tau^{\prime}
is a permutation and the mapping is injective over a finite set of size
n!
, it must also be surjective. Hence, iterating over all
\tau
’s yields all
\tau^{\prime}
’s exactly once.

Now, we have:

\displaystyle\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}}q(\tau^{\prime}(X^{\prime}))}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})))}\right|\,.

As observed in the proof of Lemma
3
, if
X^{\prime}
is a sample set drawn from
p^{\pi}
, then
\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})
has the same distribution as a sample set drawn from
(p^{\pi})^{\pi^{-1}}
, which is exactly
p
. Hence, we have:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})))}\right|}\right]}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim(p^{\pi})^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim p^{\otimes s},X^{\prime}\sim p^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(\tau(X))-q(\tau(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}\,.\end{split}

In the proof of replicability in Lemma
3
, we showed that the term in the last line is bounded by
\rho
. Hence, the statement of the lemma follows.
∎

5
Replicable Lower Bounds via Chaining

In this section, we introduce a general framework for proving lower bounds for replicable testing algorithms. We refer to
Section

2.2
for a high-level intuitive overview of the following theorem.

We start with the following general lemma, which we will apply to prove our chaining lower bound for discrete distributions.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\rho\in(0,0.001]
and
1\leq t\leq 1/(300\rho)
. Let
Z_{0},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t}
be distributions in some probability space
\Omega
, such that for every
1\leq i\leq t
,
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{i-1},Z_{i})\leq 0.5
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that takes a value
x\in\Omega
and randomness
r\sim\Unif[0,1]
, computes a deterministic function
h(x)\in[0,1]
and accepts if and only if
r\leq h(x)
. In other words, it satisfies the canonical property in
Definition

6
, though we think of the input as a single sample.

Then, suppose that for every
0\leq i\leq t
,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,x\sim Z_{i},x^{\prime}\sim Z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right.}{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right]}\leq\rho
. Then, either the probability that
\mathcal{A}
accepts on
Z_{0}
is less than
2/3
, or the probability that
\mathcal{A}
rejects on
Z_{t}
is less than
2/3
.

Although
Lemma

5
is stated for a single sample, we can apply it to the setting of multiple samples by letting
\Omega
be a product space.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
Definition

6
, for any distribution
Z
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,x\sim Z,x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right.}{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right.}{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right.}{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right.}{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|h(x)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right.}{h(x^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\right.}{\left|h(x)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right.}{h(x^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|h(x)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right.}{h(x^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\right]}.

(by Jensen’s inequality)

So, if
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,x\sim Z_{i},x^{\prime}\sim Z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right.}{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{(\mathcal{A}(x;r)\neq\mathcal{A}(x;r))}\right]}\leq\rho
, then by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
0.9
over
x\sim Z_{i}
,
|h(x)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right.}{h(x^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right]}|\leq 10\rho
. If we define
q_{i}:=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim Z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right.}{h(x^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x^{\prime})}\right]}
, then
|h(x)-q_{i}|\leq 10\rho
with at least
0.9
probability.

As a consequence, we claim that
|q_{i}-q_{i-1}|\leq 20\rho
for all
1\leq i\leq t
. This is because if
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{i-1},Z_{i})\leq 0.5
, then since
f
is deterministic,
d_{\textit{TV}}(h(Z_{i-1}),h(Z_{i}))\leq 0.5
. So, if
|h(x)-q_{i}|\leq 10\rho
with probability at least
0.9
for
x\sim Z_{i}
, then
|h(x)-q_{i}|\leq 10\rho
with probability at least
0.4
for
x\sim Z_{i-1}
. However,
|h(x)-q_{i-1}|\leq 10\rho
with probability at least
0.9
for
x\sim Z_{i-1}
, so with probability at least
0.3
,
|h(x)-q_{i-1}|\leq 10\rho
and
|h(x)-q_{i}|\leq 10\rho
. By the Triangle inequality,
|q_{i}-q_{i-1}|\leq 20\rho
.

Applying again the Triangle inequality for
i=1,2,\dots,t
, we have
|q_{0}-q_{t}|\leq 20\rho\cdot t\leq\frac{1}{15}
, since
t\leq\frac{1}{300\rho}
. So, either
q_{0}<\frac{2}{3}
or
q_{t}>\frac{1}{3}
. Since
q_{i}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{x\sim Z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{h(x)}\right.}{h(x)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{h(x)}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{x\sim Z_{i},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{r\leq h(x)}\right.}{r\leq h(x)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{r\leq h(x)}\right]}
equals the probability of accepting a sample from
Z_{i}
, the claim is complete.
∎

See
1.2

Proof.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that a
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
exists. Let
\mathcal{A}_{3}
be the improved version of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, which has all the canonical properties including being a
\rho
-permutation robust replicable algorithm as guaranteed by Theorem
1.1
. The construction of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
may be found in Lemma
3
. Recall that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
computes a deterministic function
h(X)\in[0,1]
and then compares it with a random threshold
r\sim\Unif[0,1]
, and accepts as long as
r\leq h(X)
.

Note that the priors
\mathcal{D}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{t}
are distributions over
[n]^{k}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is label-invariant,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim p_{0},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\sim p_{0}^{\pi},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(x;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(x;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(x;r)={accept}}\right]}\geq 1-\delta

and

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\sim p_{t},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{x\sim p_{t}^{\pi},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right]}\geq 1-\delta

for any permutation
\pi
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a prior over
p_{0}^{\pi}
over different permutations,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{0},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right]}\geq 1-\delta\geq\frac{2}{3}
and
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{t},r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={reject}}\right]}\geq 1-\delta\geq\frac{2}{3}
.

By setting
Z_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
\Omega=[n]^{k}
and applying
Lemma

5
, this is a contradiction.
∎

5.1
Lower Bound Applications

5.1.1
Coin Testing

Our first application is a lower bound for coin testing (or coin estimation), a result already proven in
[
ILPS22
]
. Here, we include it as an application of our lower bound framework and demonstrate how our approach simplifies the proof. We set
p_{0}
to be an unbiased coin and
p_{t}
to be a coin with bias
\epsilon
. We then pack
t=\Theta(1/\rho)
distributions between these two by setting the bias of the
i
-th coin to be
i\,\epsilon/t
. At this point, we follow the folklore result for showing the indistinguishability via Pinsker’s inequality.

Theorem 5.1
.

For any
\rho\leq 0.001
,
\epsilon\leq 0.25
,
any
\rho
-replicable algorithm that can distinguish an unbiased coin from one with bias of
1/2\pm\epsilon
is required to use
\Omega(1/(\rho\epsilon)^{2})
samples.

Proof.

We apply
1.2
, to the following chain of distributions. Let
t\coloneqq\left\lfloor{1/300\rho)}\right\rfloor
.

\displaystyle\text{For }i\in\{0,\ldots,t\}:\quad\quad\quad p_{i}(\text{head})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\,,\text{ and }\quad\quad p_{i}(\text{tail})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\,.

Next, we show indistinguishable with
s=o(1/(\rho\epsilon)^{2})
samples.
Information theoretically, distinguishing two distributions
p_{i}
and
p_{i-1}
with
s
samples is possible with some high constant probability only when the total variation distance between
p_{i}^{\otimes s}
and
p_{i-1}^{\otimes s}
is at least a constant. Thus, we have:

\displaystyle\Theta(1)

\displaystyle\leq\|p_{i}^{\otimes s}-p_{i-1}^{\otimes s}\|_{\text{TV}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}KL(p_{i}^{\otimes s}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ p_{i-1}^{\otimes s})}

(By Pinsker’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{s}{2}KL(p_{i}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ p_{i-1})}\,.

(since samples are i.i.d.)

It suffices to show that the
KL(p_{i}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ p_{i-1})
is
O(\epsilon^{2}/t^{2})
.

\displaystyle KL(p_{i}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ p_{i-1})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\right)\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\right]+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\right)\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\right]+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{4}-\left(\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{4}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}\right]+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\cdot\log\left[\frac{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\epsilon}{2\,t}-\frac{i\,(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}}{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\epsilon}{2\,t}-\frac{i\,(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log\left[1-\frac{\left(\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{4}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}\right]+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\cdot\log\left[1+\frac{\frac{\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\epsilon}{2\,t}-\frac{i\,(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}}\right]\,.

To remove the
\log
terms, we upper bound
1+x
with
e^{x}
which holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We get:

\displaystyle KL(p_{i}\penalty\ ||\penalty\ p_{i-1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-1}{2}\cdot\frac{\frac{\epsilon}{t}\cdot\frac{(2\,i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{4}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}+\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}\cdot\frac{\frac{\epsilon}{t}}{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\epsilon}{2\,t}-\frac{i\,(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(i-0.5)\,\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}+\frac{i\,\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(i-0.5)\,\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}-\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\right)+\frac{0.5\,\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(i-0.5)\epsilon^{3}}{t^{3}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\right)+\cdot\frac{0.5\,\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\,\epsilon}{t}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{i\,\epsilon^{3}}{t^{3}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)\,.

In the last line, we used the fact that
i\leq t
, and
i\,\epsilon/t\leq 0.25
.
∎

5.1.2
Uniformity and Identity Testing

Our second application is a lower bound for uniformity testing, which also implies a lower bound for identity testing. In
[
LY24
]
, a lower bound for uniformity was presented that is restricted to label-invariant algorithms, which the authors refer to as “symmetric algorithms.” Their proof, similar to our framework, provides
t=O(1/\rho)
classes of distributions for which consecutive pairs are hard to distinguish. Besides the indistinguishability of consecutive pairs, their proof required an extensive argument for the “Lipschitz continuity of acceptance probability” of the algorithm.

We tighten their indistinguishability lower bound by a logarithmic factor and remove the restriction to label-invariant algorithms, making the result hold for all replicable algorithms. Specifically, our result on the canonical tester implies that if
any

\rho
-replicable algorithm exists for a symmetric property (such as uniformity), a label-invariant tester must also exist. This fact allows us to remove the assumption on the algorithm type, thereby simplifying the proof extensively.

See
1.3

Proof.

The second term in the maximum is necessary for testing an unbiased coin, as shown in
Theorem

5.1
and
[
ILPS22
]
. For the rest of this proof, we focus on the first term in the lower bound which is relevant only when
n\gg 1/\rho^{2}
.

We aim to apply our lower bound machinery introduced in
1.2
. We introduce
\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}
. Let
t=\left\lfloor{1/(300\rho)}\right\rfloor
. Similar to
[
LY24
]
, we define
p_{i}
for each
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,t\}
as follows: for every
j\in[n]
, the probability of
j
is given by

\displaystyle p_{i}(j)\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1+(i\,\epsilon/t)}{n}&\quad\quad\quad\text{if $j$ is even\,,}\\
\frac{1-(i\,\epsilon/t)}{n}&\quad\quad\quad\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

Each
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a uniform distribution over all possible permutations of
p_{i}
.
Clearly,
p_{0}
is the uniform distribution and must be accepted, while
p_{t}
is
\epsilon
-far from uniform.

Ignoring logarithmic factors, Lemma 4.3 in
[
LY24
]
establishes the indistinguishability of consecutive pairs, showing that distinguishing them requires at least
\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{n}\epsilon^{-2}\rho^{-1})
samples. Plugging this result directly into our lower bound machinery from
1.2
immediately implies the desired lower bound.

We now tighten their indistinguishability result by applying the “wishful thinking” lemma from
[
Val11
]
. First, let us define the necessary tools.

Definition 10
.

The
m
-based moments
M(a)
of a distribution
p
are

M(m)=m^{a}\sum_{i=1}^{n}p(i)^{a}.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
Val11
]
)
.

Suppose we are given two integers
m
and
n
, and
two distributions
p^{+}
and
p^{-}
such that their probabilities are at most
1/(500\,m)
. Also, assume the
m
-based moments of
p^{+}
and
p^{-}
, denoted by
M^{+}
and
M^{-}
, satisfy

\sum_{a\geq 2}\frac{\left|{M^{+}(a)-M^{-}(a)}\right|}{\left\lfloor{a/2}\right\rfloor!\cdot\sqrt{1+\max\left\{M^{+}(a),M^{-}(a)\right\}}}<\frac{1}{24}\,.

(5)

If a tester exists for a symmetric property
\mathcal{P}
such that it has to output
accept
for
p^{+}
and
reject
for
p^{-}
, then it must use more than
k
samples.

Let’s compute the
m
-based moment of each
p_{i}
:

\displaystyle M_{i}(a)\coloneqq m^{a}\cdot\left(\frac{n}{2}\cdot\frac{\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}}{n^{a}}+\frac{n}{2}\cdot\frac{\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}}{n^{a}}\right)

Consider the scenario where

\frac{1}{\epsilon^{6}\rho^{2}}\leq n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}\rho}\leq\sqrt{n}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\rho}\leq n\epsilon\,.

Let
m
be the following value:

m=c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\,\rho}\,,

for a sufficiently small constant
c
.
Clearly,
m\ll n
and
\frac{m}{n}\leq\epsilon
.

Now, by contradiction, there exists an algorithm that can distinguish
D_{i}
and
D_{i-1}
for some
i\in[t]
using
m
samples. If such an algorithm exists,
Theorem

5.2
implies that the sum described in
Equation

5
must be lower bounded by a constant:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a=2}^{\infty}\frac{M_{i}(a)-M_{i-1}(a)}{\left\lfloor{a/2}\right\rfloor!\cdot\sqrt{1+\max\left(M_{i}(a),\ M_{i-1}(a)\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a=2}^{\infty}\frac{\frac{n}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{a}\cdot\left(\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}+\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}-\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}-\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}\right)}{\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{a}}}

In the inequality above, we used that
\max\left(M_{i}(a),\ M_{i-1}(a)\right)
is at least
(n/2)\cdot(m/n)^{a}
. For
a=2
, the above term is:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{2}\left(2+2\,\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}-2-2\,\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{m}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{m\,i\,\epsilon^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\,t^{2}}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{m\,\epsilon^{2}}{\sqrt{n}\,t}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{m\,\epsilon^{2}\rho}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

For the rest of the terms, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}

\displaystyle\leq\Theta\left(\frac{m\,\epsilon^{2}\rho}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}}\ \sum_{a=3}^{\infty}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{a}\cdot\left(\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}+\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}-\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}-\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{a}\right)

The terms in the summation form four geometric series for which we have
\sum_{a\geq 3}x^{a}=x^{3}/(1-x)
for any
|x|<1
. Summing the the geometric series yields:

=\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{3/2}\cdot\left(\frac{\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}+\frac{\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)}\right)

(6)

Now, consider a function
f
parameterized by
c\in(0,1/2]
:

f_{c}(x)=\frac{(1-x)^{3}}{1-c(1+x)}-\frac{(1-x)^{3}}{1-c(1-x)}\,

Clearly,
f_{c}(0)
is zero. The derivative is:

\frac{df}{dx}(x)=\frac{2c(1-x)^{2}\left((1-c)^{2}(1-4x)+c^{2}x^{2}+2c^{2}x^{3}\right)}{\left(c(x-1)+1\right)^{2}\,(cx+c-1)^{2}},

which is positive for any
x\in[0,0.25]
. Hence,
f_{c}
is increasing in this interval. That is, for any
x<x^{\prime}
in
[0,0.25]

f_{c}(x)\leq f_{c}(x^{\prime})
. Now, we set
x=(i-1)\,\epsilon/t
and
x^{\prime}=i\epsilon/t
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq f_{\sqrt{m/n}}\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)-f_{\sqrt{m/n}}\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)}+\frac{\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)}

Using this inequality, we can bound the expression in
Equation

6
:

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{2}\,n}\cdot\left(\frac{\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}+\left(1-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}+\left(1-\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{3}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{2}\,n}\cdot\left(\frac{2+6\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)}-\frac{2+6\,\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}}{1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)}\right)

After algebraic simplification, the expression is:

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{2}\,n}\left[\frac{6\,\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)\left(\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\cdot\left(\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\cdot\left(2+6\,\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)-\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\cdot\left(2+6\,\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)\right)}{\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{2}\,n}\left[\frac{6\,\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)\left(\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{(i-1)\,\epsilon}{t}\right)^{2}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\cdot\left(2-6\,\left(\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\left(\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)-\left(\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)}{\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{i\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\left(1+\frac{(i-1)\epsilon}{t}\right)\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{m^{3/2}\epsilon^{2}}{n\,t}+\frac{m^{2}\,\epsilon}{n^{3/2}\,t}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{m^{3/2}\epsilon^{2}}{n\,t}+\frac{m^{2}\,\epsilon}{n^{3/2}\,t}\right)

Now, adding the term for
a=2
leaves us with:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\leq\Theta\left(\sqrt{n}\cdot\left(\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\,\rho+\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{3}\,\epsilon^{2}\,\rho+\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{4}\,\epsilon\,\rho\right)\right)

Given our assumption, we show that
m/n\leq\epsilon<1
. Clearly the second term is dominated by the first term. The third term is also dominated by the first one. Putting this together implies:

\frac{1}{24}\leq\Theta\left(\sqrt{n}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\right)^{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\,\rho\right)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad m=\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon^{2}\rho}\right)\,.

Hence, the proof is complete.
∎

5.1.3
Closeness Testing

To prove our lower bound, we use the machinery of wishful thinking lemma introduce in
[
Val11
]
, and its application in to proving lower bounds for closeness testing in
[
CDVV14
]
. We start by defining the
(m,m)
-based moments:

Definition 11
.

The
(m,m)
moments
M(r,s)
of a distribution pair
(p,q)
are

M(r,s)=m^{r+s}\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{r}q_{i}^{s}.

Theorem 5.3

(
[
Val11
]
)
.

If distributions
p_{1}^{+},p_{2}^{+},p_{1}^{-},p_{2}^{-}
have probabilities at most
1/1000m
and their
(m,m)
-based moments
M^{+}
and
M^{-}
satisfy

\sum_{r+s\geq 2}\frac{\left|{M^{+}(r,s)-M^{-}(r,s)}\right|}{\left\lfloor{r/2}\right\rfloor!\left\lfloor{s/2}\right\rfloor!\sqrt{1+\max\left\{M^{+}(r,s),M^{-}(r,s)\right\}}}<\frac{1}{360},

then the distribution pair
(p_{1}^{+},p_{2}^{+})
cannot be distinguished with probability
13/24
from
(p_{1}^{-},p_{2}^{-})
by a tester for a symmetric property that takes
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples from each distribution.

See
1.4

Proof.

The second and third terms in the lower bound are implied by uniformity testing
[
LY24
]
and
1.3
, and coin testing
[
ILPS22
]
, respectively.

It remains to consider the first term. Note that this term is only relevant when

\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}}\gg\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\iff n^{1/6}\gg\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2/3}\rho^{1/3}}\iff n\gg\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\rho^{2}}.

(7)

We will construct a chain of
t=\left\lfloor{1/(300\rho)}\right\rfloor
distributions that are pairwise hard to distinguish and invoke
1.2
. The construction of our lower bound is similar to
[
CDVV14
]
.
Let
b=\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}/n^{2/3}
and let
a=4/n
.
Note that
b\geq a
by
Equation

7
:

b=\frac{\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}}{n^{2/3}}\geq\frac{4\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}}{n^{2/3}\left\lparen n\varepsilon^{4}\rho^{2}\right\rparen^{1/3}}=\frac{4}{n}=a.

Observe that both
1/a
and
1/b
are much larger than
t=\Theta(1/\rho)
. Hence, without loss of generality assume
(1-\varepsilon)/b
and
1/a
are both integer and divisible by
t
. This will not affect our lower bound by more than a constant factor.

Let
A
,
B
, and
D
be three disjoint sets of size
(1-\varepsilon)/b
,
1/a
, and
1/a
respectively that are subsets of the domain
[n]
. Partition
B
and
D
into
t
sets of equal sizes, namely
B=\{B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}
, and
D=\{D_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}
.
Now, we define a series of sets
C_{0},C_{1},\ldots,C_{t}
. We define
C_{i}
to be a set of
1/a
, disjoint from
A
but overlapping with
B
and
D
:

C_{i}\coloneqq\left(\bigcup_{j={i+1}}^{t}B_{j}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}D_{j}\right)\,.

Define pairs of difficult distributions:

p=b\mathbf{1}_{A}+\varepsilon a\mathbf{1}_{B}\qquad q_{i}=b\mathbf{1}_{A}+\varepsilon a\mathbf{1}_{C_{i}}

Note that the pair,
q_{0}=p
, and
q_{t}
is the standard hard example from
[
CDVV14
]
.
Note that

\|p-q_{i}\|_{TV}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in B\triangle C}\varepsilon a=\frac{i\,\left|{B}\right|}{t}\cdot\varepsilon\,a=\frac{i}{t}\cdot\varepsilon

Let
m=\frac{c_{1}n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}}
for some constant
c_{1}
. We wish to show that this is an insufficient number of samples for distinguishing two consecutive pairs. Note that the maximum probability mass of
p
or any
q_{i}
is
b<1/1000m
as long as
c_{1}
is small enough (this is a condition of
Theorem

5.3
).

Next, we aim to show that for any
i\in[t]
many samples are required to distinguish between the pair
(p_{1}^{+},p_{2}^{+})=(p,q_{i})
and the pair
(p_{1}^{-},p_{2}^{-})=(p,q_{i+1})
.

Consider the
(m,m)
moments of both pairs of distributions using
u=r+s
, and set
\alpha=i/t
and
\Delta=1/t
:

\displaystyle M^{+}(r,s)

\displaystyle=m^{u}\left\lparen\frac{1-\varepsilon}{b}\right\rparen b^{u}+m^{u}\left\lparen\frac{1-\alpha}{a}\right\rparen\varepsilon^{u}a^{u}

\displaystyle=m^{u}\left\lparen 1-\varepsilon\right\rparen b^{u-1}+m^{u}\left\lparen 1-\alpha\right\rparen\varepsilon^{u}a^{u-1}

\displaystyle=m^{u}\left\lparen(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\varepsilon^{4u/3-4/3}\rho^{2u/3-2/3}}{n^{2u/3-2/3}}+(1-\alpha)\frac{4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u}}{n^{u-1}}\right\rparen

and

M^{-}(r,s)=m^{u}\left\lparen(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\varepsilon^{4u/3-4/3}\rho^{2u/3-2/3}}{n^{2u/3-2/3}}+(1-\alpha-\Delta)\frac{4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u}}{n^{u-1}}\right\rparen.

Recall that
\Delta=\Theta(\rho)
. We will focus on the following key term in
Theorem

5.3
:

\displaystyle\frac{\left|{M^{+}(r,s)-M^{-}(r,s)}\right|}{\sqrt{1+\max\left\{M^{+}(r,s),M^{-}(r,s)\right\}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{u}\Delta 4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u}/n^{u-1}}{\sqrt{1+m^{u}(1-\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{4u/3-4/3}\rho^{2u/3-2/3}/n^{2u/3-2/3}+m^{u}(1-\alpha)4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u}/n^{u-1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m^{u}\Delta 4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u}/n^{u-1}}{\sqrt{m^{u}(1-\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{4u/3-4/3}\rho^{2u/3-2/3}/n^{2u/3-2/3}}}

\displaystyle\leq O\left\lparen\frac{m^{u/2}\rho 4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u/3+2/3}}{n^{2u/3-2/3}\rho^{u/3-1/3}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O\left\lparen\frac{m^{u/2}4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u/3+2/3}}{n^{2u/3-2/3}\rho^{u/3-4/3}}\right\rparen.

Note the following inequality which is a consequence of
Equation

7
when
u\geq 2
:

n\gg\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\rho^{2}}\implies\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{\varepsilon\rho^{2}}{4}\implies\frac{1}{n^{u/3-2/3}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{u/3-2/3}\rho^{2u/3-4/3}}{4^{u/3-2/3}}.

Using this inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{\left|{M^{+}(r,s)-M^{-}(r,s)}\right|}{\sqrt{1+\max\left\{M^{+}(r,s),M^{-}(r,s)\right\}}}

\displaystyle\leq O\left\lparen\frac{m^{u/2}4^{u-1}\varepsilon^{u/3+2/3}}{n^{u/3}\rho^{u/3-4/3}}\left\lparen\frac{\varepsilon^{u/3-2/3}\rho^{2u/3-4/3}}{4^{u/3-2/3}}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{m^{u/2}4^{2u/3-1/3}\varepsilon^{2u/3}\rho^{u/3}}{n^{u/3}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen c_{1}^{u/2}4^{2u/3-1/3}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\left\lparen 4^{2/3}\cdot\sqrt{c_{1}}\right\rparen^{u}\right\rparen

For a sufficiently small
c_{1}
, the above function is exponentially decreasing in
u
. Then, one can argue the sum of all moments converges to a value less than
1/360
as desired due to the convergence of the geometric series.
The result follows directly from the argument in
[
CDVV14
]
(see Proposition 9).
∎

6
Expectation-Gap Replicable Testers

We generalize and quantitatively improve bounds for replicable expectation-gap estimators.

6.1
General Expectation-Gap Estimator

Definition 12

(Expectation-Gap Statistic)
.

Consider a hypothesis testing problem with a null hypothesis
H_{0}
(e.g., the distribution belongs to a property) and alternate hypothesis
H_{1}
(the distribution is far from the property).
Let
s
be the number of samples taken from the distribution.
Then, an expectation-gap statistic is defined by a test statistic
Z(s)
of the samples with the following properties given by real-valued functions
\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s),\sigma(s)
defined as follows:

•

Null threshold (upper bound):
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{0}}\right.}{Z(s)|H_{0}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{0}}\right]}\leq\tau_{0}(s)
.

•

Alternative threshold (lower bound):
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{1}}\right.}{Z(s)|H_{1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{1}}\right]}\geq\tau_{1}(s)
. We require that for some
s_{\min}\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
s\geq s_{\min}
,
\tau_{1}(s)\geq\tau_{0}(s)
.

•

Variance upper bound:
\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sigma(s)\left\lparen 1+\max\left\{0,\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)},\frac{\tau_{0}(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\Delta(s)}\right\}\right\rparen
.
If
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\in[\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s)]
, the condition is simply
\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sigma(s)
.
6
6

6

Importantly, we do not simply use a uniform bound on the variance. Numerous applications of expectation-gap style analyses allow for the variance to increase when the expectation of the statistic is far from the interval between the null and alternate thresholds.

The following quantities summarize the important properties of the test statistic
Z
:

•

Threshold gap:
\Delta(s)=\tau_{1}(s)-\tau_{0}(s)
. Note that this is a lower bound on the true gap
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{1}}\right.}{Z(s)|H_{1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{1}}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{0}}\right.}{Z(s)|H_{0}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)|H_{0}}\right]}
.

•

Noise-to-signal ratio:
f(s)=\frac{\sigma(s)}{\Delta(s)}
.

•

Sampling breakpoints
s_{t}
defined for any
t\in(0,1]
:

s_{t}=\min\{s\in\mathbb{N}:s\geq s_{\min}\text{ and }f(s)\leq t/2\}.

(8)

A core primitive will be bounding deviations of the expectation-gap statistic via Chebyshev’s inequality.

Lemma 6
.

Let
Z(s),\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s),\sigma(s)
be the parameters of an expectation-gap statistic.
Consider any
s\geq s_{\min}
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
If
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\in[\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s)]
, then

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\right.}{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\right]}\leq\frac{f(s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

If
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}>\tau_{1}(s)
, then

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\right.}{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\right]}\leq\frac{f(s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

If
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}<\tau_{0}(s)
, then

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)\geq\tau_{0}(s)+\alpha\Delta(s)}\right.}{Z(s)\geq\tau_{0}(s)+\alpha\Delta(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)\geq\tau_{0}(s)+\alpha\Delta(s)}\right]}\leq\frac{f(s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

Proof.

First, consider the case where
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\in[\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s)]
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\right.}{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\alpha^{2}\Delta(s)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{f(s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

Now, consider the case where
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}>\tau_{1}(s)
. The case where the expectation is less than
\tau_{0}(s)
is symmetric.

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\right.}{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)\leq\tau_{1}(s)-\alpha\Delta(s)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}\right.}{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{Z(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s)+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\Delta(s)^{2}\left\lparen\alpha+\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)}\right\rparen^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma(s)^{2}\left\lparen 1+\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)}\right\rparen^{2}}{\Delta(s)^{2}\left\lparen\alpha+\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)}\right\rparen^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{f(s)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

∎

When
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\in[\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s)]
, at the sampling breakpoints,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{Z(s_{t})-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right.}{Z(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right]}}\right|\geq t\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{\left|{Z(s_{t})-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right.}{Z(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right]}}\right|\geq t\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{Z(s_{t})-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right.}{Z(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right]}}\right|\geq t\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}\leq\frac{1}{4}.

(9)

For example, taking
s_{0.5}
samples and thresholding at
\tau_{0}(s_{0.5})+\Delta(s_{0.5})/2
would yield a standard non-replicable tester with constant success probability.

Example 1
.

In the (unbiased) coin testing problem, samples are drawn from a
\mathrm{Ber}(p)
distribution. The null hypothesis is that
p=1/2
and the alternative hypothesis is that
p\geq 1/2+\varepsilon
for a parameter
\varepsilon>0
.
The test statistic
Z(s)
is the number of sampled elements which are heads.

•

A valid null and alternative threshold are
\tau_{0}(s)=s/2
and
\tau_{1}(s)=s/2+\varepsilon s
, respectively, with
s_{\min}=0
.

•

A variance upper bound is given by
\sigma(s)=\sqrt{s}/2
.

•

The threshold gap and noise-to-signal ratio are
\Delta(s)=\varepsilon s
and
f(s)=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon\sqrt{s}}
, respectively.

•

The sampling breakpoints are therefore
s_{t}=\left\lceil{\frac{1}{t^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}}\right\rceil
.

Algorithm 6.1: General Expectation-Gap Estimator

1.

Pick
t\in[\rho,1/16]
.

2.

Sample
r\sim\Unif\left\lparen\left[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right]\right\rparen
.

3.

Repeat
L=O\left\lparen\frac{t^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right\rparen
times: take
s_{t}
samples and compute the statistic
\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[L]
.

4.

Compute the median estimate
\hat{V}=\text{median}\left\lparen\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{L}\right\rparen
.

5.

Output
accept
if
\hat{V}<\tau_{0}(s_{t})+\Delta(s_{t})/8
and
reject
if
\hat{V}>\tau_{1}(s_{t})-\Delta(s_{t})/8
. Otherwise, continue.

6.

Compute the mean estimate
\hat{W}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{\ell}
.

7.

Output
accept
if
\hat{W}\leq\tau_{0}(s_{t})+r\Delta(s_{t})
and
reject
otherwise.

Theorem 6.1
.

Given parameters
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
,
Section

6.1
for a hypothesis testing problem
(H_{0},H_{1})
defined by
Z(s),\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s),\sigma(s)
and a parameter
t\in[\rho,1/16]
and a constant
C
has the following properties:

•

The algorithm takes
s=O\left\lparen\frac{s_{t}t^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right\rparen
samples.

•

The algorithm succeeds with probability at least
1-t^{Ct^{2}/\rho^{2}}
.

•

The algorithm is
\rho
-replicable.

Remark 1
.

For any application,
t
should be optimized given the sampling breakpoints of the given estimator and desired sample complexity/failure probability tradeoff. In the two extremes of the setting of
t
, the failure probability can be as small as
\exp\left\lparen-\Omega(1/\rho^{2})\right\rparen
when
t=O(1)
or as large as
\rho^{C}
when
t=\rho
. In either case, the algorithm succeeds with high probability in
1/\rho
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

The sample complexity of the algorithm is immediate.
Let
X
be a random variable representing the set of samples collected by the algorithm. Let
C^{\prime}
be such that
L\leq C^{\prime}t^{2}/\rho^{2}
.

Correctness

Correctness of the algorithm is guaranteed by the median estimate.
Let
z=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right.}{Z(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right]}
be the expectation of the statistic.
Recall that in the null hypothesis,
z\leq\tau_{0}(s_{t})
, and in the alternate hypothesis,
z\geq\tau_{1}(s_{t})
.
The algorithm is correct if it outputs
accept
and
reject
in these two cases, respectively.

We will show that the median estimate
\hat{V}
is contained within an interval of length
\Delta(s_{t})/4
around
z
with high probability.
Recall from
Lemma

6
, we get Chebyshev-style bounds within the interval
[\tau_{0}(s_{t}),\tau_{1}(s_{t})]
regardless of the location of
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}
. We will only be concerned with deviations within this interval and assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right.}{Z(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s_{t})}\right]}\in[\tau_{0}(s_{t}),\tau_{1}(s_{t})]
.
By
Lemma

6
and the definition of
s_{t}
(
Equation

8
):

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{Z(s_{t})-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\right.}{\left|{Z(s_{t})-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{Z(s_{t})-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\right]}\leq 64f(s_{t})^{2}\leq 16t^{2}.

As we choose
t\leq 1/16
, this probability is at most
1/16
.

By a standard median analysis via a Chernoff bound, the probability that
\hat{V}
deviates from
z
decays exponentially:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\right.}{\left|{\hat{V}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf{1}\left[\left|{\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{\ell}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8\right]\geq L/2}\right.}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf{1}\left[\left|{\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{\ell}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8\right]\geq L/2}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf{1}\left[\left|{\hat{Z}(s_{t})_{\ell}-z}\right|\geq\Delta(s_{t})/8\right]\geq L/2}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{A\sim\mathrm{Bin}(L,16t^{2})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq L/4}\right.}{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq L/4}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq L/4}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{A\sim\mathrm{Bin}(L,16t^{2})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{64t^{2}}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right.}{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{64t^{2}}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{A-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{64t^{2}}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A}\right.}{A}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lparen\frac{e^{1/64t^{2}}}{(1+1/64t^{2})^{1+1/64t^{2}}}\right\rparen^{16t^{2}L}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lparen\frac{e}{1+1/64t^{2}}\right\rparen^{\frac{C^{\prime}t^{2}}{4\rho^{2}}}.

For any constant
C
as given in the theorem statement, for a large enough constant
C^{\prime}
, the probability that the median
\hat{V}
deviates from
z
by more than
\Delta(s_{t})/8
can be upper bounded by
t^{Ct^{2}/\rho^{2}}
.
By thresholding above at
\tau_{0}(s_{t})+\Delta(s_{t})/8
and below at
\tau_{1}(s_{t})-\Delta(s_{t})/8
, we ensure correctness under
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
with probability
1-t^{Ct^{2}/\rho^{2}}
.

Replicability

Replicability must be satisfied even if neither
H_{0}
nor
H_{1}
holds.
In this case, the standard hypothesis testing definition does not specify a correct answer between
accept
and
reject
.
In order to prove replicability, we will chose a correct answer as follows.
We say that the algorithm should
accept
if
z\leq\tau_{0}+r\Delta(s_{t})
and
reject
otherwise.
We will show that, with probability
1-\rho
over the randomness of
r
and the sampling process, the algorithm will return the “correct” answer with probability
1-\rho
.
By union bound over resampling with the same draw of
r
, the algorithm is
2\rho
-replicable. We will proceed by cases over the location of
z
.

First, consider the case that the algorithm terminates on the median comparison of
\hat{V}
.
By the correctness analysis,
\hat{V}
is concentrated within
\Delta(s_{t})/8
of
z
with high probability in
1/\rho
.
Therefore, the median comparison will only
accept
if
z<\tau_{0}(s_{t})+\Delta(s_{t})/4
and will only
reject
if
z>\tau_{1}(s_{t})-\Delta(s_{t})/4
.
Since we choose
r\in\left[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right]
, this step will only terminate with the correct answer.

If the median comparison does not return decisively, the output of the algorithm is determined by the mean comparison of
\hat{W}
.
Note that
\hat{W}
is the mean of
L
independent estimators with expectation
z
and standard deviation at most
\sigma(s_{t})\leq f(s_{t})\Delta(s_{t})\leq t\Delta(s_{t})/2
(the last step uses the definition of
s_{t}
in
Equation

8
).
It follows that the mean of
\hat{W}
is
z
and its variance is
t^{2}\Delta(s_{t})^{2}/4L
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq\alpha\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}\leq\frac{t^{2}\Delta(s_{t})^{2}}{4L\alpha^{2}\Delta(s_{t})^{2}}=\frac{\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}\alpha^{2}}.

(10)

The algorithm outputs the incorrect answer if
\hat{W}
is on the wrong side (i.e., not the same side as
z
) of the random threshold defined by
r
. The probability that this occurs is upper bounded by the probability that
\hat{W}
deviates from its expectation by more than the distance between
\tau_{0}(s_{t})+r\Delta(s_{t})
and
z
.
Let
D(r)=\left|{\tau_{0}(s_{t})+r\Delta(s_{t})-z}\right|
be a random variable (over the randomness of
r
) for this distance.
As
r
is sampled uniformly in
\left[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right]
independently of
z
, the probability that
D(r)\leq\beta\Delta(s_{t})
is upper bounded by the probability that
r
lands in an interval of length
2\beta
.
This probability is at most
4\beta
.
Then, the probability of a replicability failure is upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right.}{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})\text{ and }\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-(k+1)},2^{-k}\right]\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k+2}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-(k+1)}\Delta(s_{t})}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k+2}\min\left\{\frac{\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}2^{-2(k+1)}},1\right\}

(by
Equation

10
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\min\left\{\frac{2^{k}\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}},2^{-k+2}\right\}.

Consider the case when the second term in the minimization dominates:

\frac{2^{k}\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}}\geq 2^{-k+2}\iff 2^{2k}\geq\frac{16C^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}\iff k\geq\frac{1}{2}\lg\frac{16C^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}.

Then, the probability of a replicability failure is at most

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}-z}\right|\geq D(r)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\frac{1}{2}\lg\frac{16C^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}}\right\rceil-1}\frac{2^{k}\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}}+\sum_{k=\left\lceil{\frac{1}{2}\lg\frac{16C^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}}\right\rceil}^{\infty}2^{-k+2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{16C^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}}\left\lparen\frac{\rho^{2}}{4C^{\prime}}\right\rparen+8\sqrt{\frac{\rho^{2}}{16C^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\rho}{\sqrt{C^{\prime}}}.

Choosing a large enough constant
C^{\prime}
suffices for
\rho
-replicability.
∎

6.2
Size-Invariant Expectation-Gap Estimator

In this subsection, we define a special type of expectation-gap statistics (generalizing coin testers and collision-based testers) where, under some normalization, the expectation of the statistic as well as the null and alternate thresholds are constant with respect to the number of samples.
For such statistics, we design a general estimator which also gives improved bounds on the number of samples taken
in expectation
.

Definition 13

(Size-Invariant Expectation-Gap Statistics)
.

An expectation-gap statistic defined by
Z(s),\allowbreak\tau_{0}(s),\tau_{1}(s),\sigma(s)
(see
Definition

12
) is “size-invariant” if there exist fixed values
z,\tau_{0},\tau_{1}
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}=z
,
\tau_{0}(s)=\tau_{0}
, and
\tau_{1}(s)=\tau_{1}
.
In words, the location of the test statistic as well as the expectation thresholds do not vary with the number of samples.
We parameterize such a statistic by
Z(s),\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\sigma(s)
and define
\Delta
,
f(s)
, and sampling breakpoints
s_{t}
analogously to
Definition

12
.

Example 2
.

For the coin testing problem, a size-invariant expectation gap statistic is given by
Z(s)
being the fraction of heads in the sample,
\tau_{0}=1/2
,
\tau_{1}=1/2+\varepsilon
, and
\sigma(s)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{s}}
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Given parameters
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
,
Section

6.2
for a hypothesis testing problem
(H_{0},H_{1})
with a size-invariance expectation gap statistic defined by
Z(s),\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\sigma(s)
and a sequence of breakpoints given by
t_{1},\ldots,t_{K}
has the following properties:

•

The algorithm succeeds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

•

The algorithm is
O(\rho)
-replicable.

•

The algorithm takes

O\left\lparen s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{k}(K-k+1)t_{k}^{2}s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen

samples in expectation, and

O\left\lparen s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{2k}(K-k+1)t_{k}^{2}s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen

samples in the worst-case.

Algorithm 6.2: Size-Invariant Expectation-Gap Estimator

1.

Repeat
L=\left\lceil{8\ln(1/\delta)}\right\rceil
times: take
s_{1/8}
samples and compute the statistic
\hat{Z}(s_{1/8})
. Compute the median of these estimates. Output
accept
if the median is less than
\tau_{0}+\Delta/8
and
reject
if it is greater than
\tau_{1}-\Delta/8
. Otherwise, continue.

2.

Pick
r\sim\Unif\left\lparen\left[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right]\right\rparen
.

3.

Let
K=\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil
. For
k\in[K]
:

(a)

Choose
t_{k}\in[2^{-k},1/2]
.

(b)

Repeat
J_{k}=\left\lceil{t_{k}^{2}2^{2k}}\right\rceil
times: take
s_{t_{k}}
samples and compute the test statistic
\hat{Z}(s_{t_{k}})
. Call the mean of these test statistics
\hat{W}_{k}
.

(c)

Repeat the preceding step
L_{k}=16(K-k+1)
times and take the median-of-means estimate of the
\hat{W}_{k}
’s, call this quantity
\hat{V}_{k}
.

(d)

Output
accept
if
\hat{V}_{k}\leq\tau_{0}+\left\lparen r-2^{-k}\right\rparen\Delta
and
reject
if
\hat{V}_{k}\geq\tau_{0}+\left\lparen r+2^{-k}\right\rparen\Delta
. Otherwise, continue.

4.

Output
accept
.

The flexibility of choosing
t_{1},\ldots t_{K}
allows the estimator to leverage an analysis of estimator’s variance beyond the black-box sample mean bounds (see new results on uniformity testing in
Section

6.3.2
).
We present the following corollary which gives a simpler expression when no such analysis exists (or when the basic analysis is tight as in coin testing in
Section

6.3.1
).

Corollary 6.1
.

Given parameters
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
, there exists an algorithm for a hypothesis testing problem
(H_{0},H_{1})
with a size-invariant expectation gap statistic defined by
Z(s),\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\sigma(s)
which is
\rho
-replicable, fails with probability at most
\min\{\delta,\exp(-1/\rho)\}
, and takes samples
O\left\lparen\frac{s_{1/8}}{\rho}+s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)\right\rparen
in expectation and
O\left\lparen\frac{s_{1/8}}{\rho^{2}}+s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)\right\rparen
in the worst-case.

Proof.

The corollary follows from applying
Theorem

6.2
with
t_{1},\ldots,t_{K}
all set to
1/8
and
\delta=\exp\left\lparen 1/\rho\right\rparen
.
As
t_{k}
and
s_{t_{k}}
are fixed, the summations in the sample complexity (both expected and worst-case) are dominated by the final term with
k=\left\lceil{\log(1/\rho)}\right\rceil
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.2
.

Let
X
be a random variable representing the samples collected by the algorithm.
Recall that
z=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}
is a constant due to the size-invariant property of the statistic.

Correctness

The correctness of the algorithm is achieved in the first step. Note that correctness only needs to hold in the case that the true distribution belongs to the null hypothesis
H_{0}
or the alternate hypothesis
H_{1}
. Assume without loss of generality that we are in the former case.

Recall from
Lemma

6
, we get Chebyshev-style bounds within the interval
[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
