regardless of the location of
z
. We will only be concerned with deviations within this interval and assume without loss of generality that
z\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
. By
Lemma

6
and
Equation

9
, a single estimate
\hat{Z}(s_{1/8})
will deviate from its expectation
\mathbb{E}[Z(s_{1/8}|H_{0}]\leq\tau_{0}
by more than
\Delta/8
with probability at most
1/4
. By the standard median-of-means analysis, the probability that the median of
L
estimates of
\hat{Z}(s_{1/8})
will deviate from the expectation by more than
\Delta/8
is at most the probability that the sum of
L

\mathrm{Ber}(1/4)
i.i.d. random variables exceeds
L/2
.
By Hoeffding’s bound, this occurs with probability at most
\exp\left\lparen-\frac{2(L/4)^{2}}{L}\right\rparen=\exp\left\lparen-L/8\right\rparen
.
By choosing
L=8\ln(1/\delta)
and outputting
accept
if the median-of-means estimate is less than
\tau_{0}(s_{1/8})+\Delta(s_{1/8})/8
, the algorithm only fails (does not output
accept
) with probability at most
\delta
.

Sample Complexity

The worst-case sample complexity comes from a simple summation of the number of samples used if the algorithm does not terminate early.

For the analysis of the expected sample size, we will need to analyze the quality of the median-of-means estimates
\hat{V}_{k}
.
First, consider a single mean estimate
\hat{W}_{k}
.
As it is the mean of independent random variables with standard deviation
\sigma(s_{t})\leq t\Delta/2
, the expectation of
\hat{W}_{k}
is
z
and its variance is
t^{2}\Delta^{2}/4J_{k}
. Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\right.}{\left|{\hat{W}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{W}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\right]}\leq\frac{t^{2}\Delta^{2}2^{2k}}{4J_{k}\Delta^{2}}\leq\frac{t^{2}2^{2k}}{4(t_{k}^{2}2^{2k})}=1/4.

By the standard median-of-means Hoeffding bound for the median of
L_{k}
such estimates,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\right.}{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-z}\right|\geq 2^{-k}\Delta}\right]}\leq\exp\left\lparen-L_{k}/8\right\rparen.

(11)

Consider the random variable
D(r)=\left|{z-(\tau_{0}+r\Delta)}\right|
which is the distance between the random threshold and the expectation of the statistic.
Let
A_{k}
be a binary random variable for the event that the algorithm has not terminated at the end of step
k
.
The algorithm will have terminated at the end of step
k
if the estimate
\hat{V}_{k^{\prime}}
is far from the random threshold
r
for any
k^{\prime}\leq k
.
The quantity of how far
\hat{V}_{k^{\prime}}
must be from
r
in order to terminate decreases with
k^{\prime}
.
The probability of the event
A_{k}
is upper bounded by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A_{k}}\right.}{A_{k}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A_{k}}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-(\tau_{0}+r\Delta)}\right|<2^{-k}\Delta)}\right.}{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-(\tau_{0}+r\Delta)}\right|<2^{-k}\Delta)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{k}-(\tau_{0}+r\Delta)}\right|<2^{-k}\Delta)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\left\lparen\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-k^{\prime}},2^{-k^{\prime}+1}\right]\Delta}\right.}{D(r)\in\left[2^{-k^{\prime}},2^{-k^{\prime}+1}\right]\Delta}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{D(r)\in\left[2^{-k^{\prime}},2^{-k^{\prime}+1}\right]\Delta}\right]}\prod_{\ell=k^{\prime}}^{k}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{\ell}-z]}\right|>2^{-k^{\prime}}\Delta)}\right.}{\left|{\hat{V}_{\ell}-z]}\right|>2^{-k^{\prime}}\Delta)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{\hat{V}_{\ell}-z]}\right|>2^{-k^{\prime}}\Delta)}\right]}\right\rparen+\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{D(r)>2^{-k+1}}\right.}{D(r)>2^{-k+1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{D(r)>2^{-k+1}}\right]}.

The inequality follows from conditioning on the geometric interval (defined by
k^{\prime}
) which contains
D(r)
.
Conditioned on this interval, the algorithm only does not terminate by step
k
only if every estimate of
\hat{V}_{\ell}
for
\ell\leq k
deviates from its expectation by more than the lower endpoint of this interval, which is
2^{-k^{\prime}}\Delta
.

Recall from the proof of
Theorem

6.1
that the probability that
D(r)\leq\beta\Delta
is upper bounded by
4\beta
(simply from the probability of the uniform random variable
r
landing in a
2\beta
sized interval).
Combined with
Equation

11
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A_{k}}\right.}{A_{k}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A_{k}}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\left\lparen 2^{-k^{\prime}+3}\prod_{\ell=k^{\prime}}^{k}\exp(-L_{k}/8)\right\rparen+2^{-k+3}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\left\lparen 2^{-k^{\prime}+3}\exp\left\lparen-\sum_{\ell=k^{\prime}}^{k}L_{k}/8\right\rparen\right\rparen+2^{-k+3}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\left\lparen 2^{-k^{\prime}+3}2^{-2(K-k^{\prime}+1)}\right\rparen+2^{-k+3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}2^{-2K+k^{\prime}+1}+2^{-k+3}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-2K+k+1}+2^{-k+3}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-k+4}.

(12)

The expected number of samples is equal to the samples taken in each step times the probability that the algorithm reaches that step.
For simplicity, define
A_{0}=1
deterministically.
Ignoring the
8s_{1/8}\ln(1/\delta)
samples taken at the beginning of the algorithm, the expected number of samples is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{A_{k-1}}\right.}{A_{k-1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{A_{k-1}}\right]}J_{k}L_{k}s_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}2^{-k+4}\left\lceil{t_{k}^{2}2^{2k}}\right\rceil\left\lceil{16(K-k+1)}\right\rceil s_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{k}(K-k+1)t_{k}^{2}s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen.

The final bound follows by including the
8s_{1/8}\ln(1/\delta)
samples taken deterministically at the beginning of the algorithm.

Replicability

As in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
, we will define a notion of replicable correctness (which depends on the randomness
r
) which is defined even if the underlying distribution satisfies neither the null nor alternate hypothesis.
As long as the algorithm is correct in this notion with probability
1-\rho
, it will overall be
2\rho
-replicable.
As in the prior proof, we will say that the algorithm should output
accept
if
z\leq\tau_{0}+r\Delta
and
reject
otherwise.

The algorithm only outputs the wrong answer in the first step if the median estimate is smaller than
\tau_{0}+\Delta/8
or larger than
\tau_{1}+\Delta/8
.
To show correctness (in the standard, non-replicable) sense, we showed that the median deviates from
z
by more than
\Delta/8
only with probability
\delta\leq\rho
.
As
r\in\left\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right\}
, with probability
1-\rho
, if the algorithm terminates in the first step, it is replicably correct as it must be the case that
z<\tau_{0}+\Delta/4
or
z>\tau_{0}+3\Delta/4
.

Now, consider the case that the algorithm does not terminate in the first step.
As in the proof of expected sample complexity, we will break down the failure probability of the algorithm depending on the event
A_{k}
that the algorithm does has not terminated by the end of step
k
.
Let
B_{k}
be the event that the algorithm outputs the replicably incorrect answer at step
k
.
At step
k<K
, the algorithm only terminates if
\hat{V}_{k}
is
2^{-k}\Delta
far from the random threshold
\tau_{0}+r\Delta
.
As the definition of replicable correctness is based on the location of
z
relative to the threshold, the algorithm only terminates incorrectly if
\hat{V}_{k}
is
2^{-k}\Delta
far from
z
.
By
Equation

11
, this occurs with probability at most
\exp(-L_{k}/8)
.
Note that this deviation event is independent of
A_{k-1}
, the event of the algorithm having not terminated up to this point.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{B_{k}}\right.}{B_{k}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{B_{k}}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq A_{k-1}\exp(-L_{k}/8)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-k+5}e^{-2(K-k+1)}

(by
Equation

12
)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-2K+k+4}.

By union bound, the failure probability across of the steps is upper bounded by

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{B_{k}}\right.}{B_{k}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{B_{k}}\right]}\leq 2^{-2K+4}\sum_{k=1}^{k}2^{k}\leq 2^{-K+5}=O(\rho).

Overall, the algorithm is
O(\rho)
-replicable, as required.
∎

Remark 2

(Importance of Size-Invariance)
.

The size-invariant property is key to the replicability of our algorithm.
Replicable correctness is defined by comparing
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}
to the random threshold defined by
r
.
If the location of
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}
changes with the number of samples across different levels of the algorithm, then two independent runs of the algorithm may terminate on different levels with a different notion of replicable correctness.
This would violate replicability.

6.3
Upper Bound Applications

6.3.1
Coin Testing

We apply our expectation-gap framework to the fundamental replicable coin testing problem which is ubiquitous as a subroutine in replicable algorithms.
Our algorithm improves the sample complexity both in expectation and in the worst-case over existing bounds and
matches lower bounds in all parameter regimes up to constant factors
.
The proof of this result is a direct application of
Theorem

6.2
.

Definition 14

(Replicable (Biased) Coin Testing (as defined in
[
HIK+24
]
))
.

Consider
0\leq p_{0}<q_{0}\leq 1
and let
\varepsilon=q_{0}-p_{0}
. For a
p\in[0,1]
, an algorithm receives samples from
\mathrm{Ber}(p)
. The null hypothesis is that
p=p_{0}
and the alterate hypothesis is that
p\geq q_{0}
.

Prior work:

Recall that the prior state-of-the-art sample complexity was given in
[
HIK+24
]
.

Theorem 6.3

(Theorem 3.5 of
[
HIK+24
]
)
.

For any
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
, there exists an algorithm for replicable coin testing which is
\rho
-replicable, fails with probability at most
\delta
, and uses samples
O\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen
in expectation and
O\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen
in the worst-case.

Lower bounds (with and without replicability) appear in
[
HIK+24
]
and
[
LV21
]
.

Theorem 6.4

(Theorem 3.7 of
[
HIK+24
]
)
.

Consider any
p_{0}<q_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta\leq\rho\leq 1/16
. Any replicable coin testing algorithm with these parameters must use
\Omega\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen
samples in expectation and
\Omega\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen
in the worst-case.

Theorem 6.5

(Direct Corollary of Theorem 1.3 of
[
LV21
]
)
.

Consider any
p_{0}\in[0,1/2)
and
q_{0}>p_{0}
where
q_{0}-p_{0}\leq 1-2p_{0}
. Any (non-replicable) algorithm which solves the coin testing problem must use
\Omega\left\lparen\frac{p_{0}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen
samples in expectation.

Our result:

Our result improves upon the prior best upper bound and matches the lower bounds up to constant factors for the sample complexity in the worst-case and in expectation.

7
7

7

Note that the non-replicable lower bound has
p_{0}
in the numerator rather than
q_{0}=p_{0}+\varepsilon
which introduces an extra
\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}
additive term in our upper bound. This is necessary even if
p_{0}=0
as with
s=o\left\lparen\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen
from
\mathrm{Ber}(\varepsilon)
, no heads appear in the sample with probability greater than
\delta
and thus it is impossible to distinguish from sampling from
\mathrm{Ber}(0)
.

Theorem 6.6
.

For any
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
,
Section

6.2
solves replicable coin testing.
It is
\rho
-replicable, fails with probability at most
\min\{\delta,\exp(-1/\rho)\}
, and uses samples
O\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}+\frac{q_{0}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen
in expectation and
O\left\lparen\frac{q_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{q_{0}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen
in the worst-case.

Proof.

Consider the size-invariant expectation-gap statistic statistic
Z(s)
which is the fraction of samples which are heads.
As
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}=p
,
\tau_{0}=p_{0}
and
\tau_{1}=q_{0}
are valid null and alternate thresholds, respectively.
Therefore,
\Delta=\varepsilon\leq 1
.
The variance of the estimator is
\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}=p(1-p)/s\leq p/s
. Note that if
p>q_{0}
,

\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{s}}=\sqrt{\frac{q_{0}}{s}\left\lparen 1+\frac{p-q_{0}}{q_{0}}\right\rparen}\leq\sqrt{\frac{q_{0}}{s}\left\lparen 1+\frac{p-q_{0}}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen}\leq\sqrt{\frac{q_{0}}{s}}\left\lparen 1+\frac{p-q_{0}}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen.

Therefore,
\sigma(s)=\sqrt{q_{0}/s}
is a valid variance upper bound.
The resulting noise-to-signal ratio is
\frac{\sqrt{q_{0}}}{\varepsilon\sqrt{s}}
, and the constant sampling breakpoint is achieved at
s_{1/8}=O(q_{0}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

Applying
Corollary

6.1
with this statistic yields the result.
∎

6.3.2
Uniformity Testing

Prior work:

The work of
[
LY24
]
introduces the problem of replicable uniformity testing (see
Definition

2
) and gave the following upper bound.

Theorem 6.7

(Theorem 1.3 of
[
LY24
]
)
.

Consider
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\rho\leq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
.
There exists an algorithm which solves
(n,\varepsilon,\rho,\rho)
-replicable uniformity testing and takes

O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\rho)\sqrt{\log(n/\rho)}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}+\frac{\log(1/\rho)}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen

samples in the worst-case.

Our result:

We give the first in-expectation and with high probability sampling bounds for replicable uniformity testing as a direct application of our framework.
These bounds significantly improve the
\rho
dependence from prior work and show that
replicability can be achieved for free
if
\rho\gg\frac{\varepsilon}{\log(1/\delta)}
and
n
is sufficiently large.
The worst-case sample complexity of our algorithm improves upon log factors over the prior work in some regimes though includes a
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon\rho^{2}}
term which does not appear in the preceeding theorem.

Theorem 6.8
.

Consider
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Section

6.2
solves
(n,\varepsilon,\rho,\delta)
-replicable uniformity testing, taking

O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon\rho}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen

samples in expectation and

O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen

samples in the worst-case.

Key to our result will be the well-known collision tester for uniformity testing where
Z(s)
is the number of collisions among the sampled elements divided by
\binom{s}{2}
. We will make use of the tight analysis of this statistic given by
[
DGPP19
]
.

Lemma 7

(From the proof of Lemma 7 in
[
DGPP19
]
)
.

Consider a distribution
p
over
[n]
where
\|p\|_{2}^{2}=(1+\alpha)/n
with
\alpha>0
.
Then, there exists a universal constant
C
such that:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq C\left\lparen\frac{1}{s^{2}}\left\lparen\frac{1+\alpha}{n}\right\rparen+\frac{1}{s}\left\lparen\frac{\alpha}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{3/2}}{n^{3/2}}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

Proof of
Theorem

6.8
.

Consider the size-invariant expectation-gap statistic
Z(s)
which is the number of collisions among the sampled elements divided by
\binom{s}{2}
.
Standard analysis (e.g. see
[
Can20
]
) shows
z=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}=\|p\|_{2}^{2}
.
Furthermore, if
p=\Unif([n])
,
\|p\|_{2}^{2}=1/n
, and if
\|p,\Unif[n]\|_{1}\geq\varepsilon
,
\|p\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1+\varepsilon^{2})/n
.
Therefore, setting
\tau_{0}=1/n
and
\tau_{1}=(1+\varepsilon^{2})/n
are valid choices for the null and alternate thresholds, respectively.
Then,
\Delta=\varepsilon^{2}/n
.

Let
\alpha
be such that
z=(1+\alpha)/n
.
To choose a valid variance upper bound
\sigma(s)
, it must satisfy the condition that

\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sigma(s)\left\lparen 1+\max\left\{0,\frac{z-\tau_{1}}{\Delta}\right\}\right\rparen=\sigma(s)\left\lparen 1+\max\left\{0,\frac{(\alpha-\varepsilon^{2})/n}{\varepsilon^{2}/n}\right\}\right\rparen=\sigma(s)\max\left\{1,\frac{\alpha}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}.

Using the variance analysis from
Lemma

7
which is monotonically increasing in
\alpha
, it suffices to choose
\sigma(s)
such that, for any
\alpha\geq\varepsilon^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(s)\alpha}{\varepsilon^{2}}\geq\sqrt{C\left\lparen\frac{1}{s^{2}}\left\lparen\frac{1+\alpha}{n}\right\rparen+\frac{1}{s}\left\lparen\frac{\alpha}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{3/2}}{n^{3/2}}\right\rparen\right\rparen}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma(s)\geq\frac{C_{1}\varepsilon^{2}}{\alpha m\sqrt{n}}+\frac{C_{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\left\lparen\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\alpha^{1/2}n}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\alpha^{1/4}n^{3/4}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma(s)\geq\frac{C_{1}}{s\sqrt{n}}+\frac{C_{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\left\lparen\frac{\varepsilon}{n}+\frac{\varepsilon^{3/2}}{n^{3/4}}\right\rparen.

(as
\alpha\geq\varepsilon^{2}
)

We will choose this final expression as our variance upper bound
\sigma(s)
.

The resulting noise-to-signal ratio is

f(s)=\frac{\sigma(s)}{\Delta}=\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{n}}{s\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{C_{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\left\lparen\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{n^{1/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right\rparen.

To bound the sampling breakpoints
s_{t}
, we will proceed by cases on each of the two terms of
f(s)
.
For the first term, it must be the case that

\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{n}}{s_{t}\varepsilon^{2}}\leq t/2\iff s_{t}\geq\frac{2C_{1}\sqrt{n}}{t\varepsilon^{2}}.

For the second term, it must be that

\frac{C_{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\left\lparen\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\frac{n^{1/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right\rparen\leq t/2\iff s_{t}\geq\frac{4C_{2}^{2}}{t^{2}}\left\lparen\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen.

Overall, the sampling breakpoint is

s_{t}=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}t}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon t^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}\right\rparen.

Now that we have described the size-invariant expectation-gap statistic, it remains to choose breakpoints for our algorithm.
Consider running
Section

6.2
with
t_{k}=2^{-k}
.
The algorithm is
\rho
-replicable and is correct with probability
1-\delta
.
The expected number of samples taken are

\displaystyle O

\displaystyle\left\lparen s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{k}(K-k+1)t_{k}^{2}s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{-k}(K-k+1)s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{-k}(K-k+1)\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}2^{k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}2^{2k}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{2^{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}(K-k+1)\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}2^{k}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{2^{k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\rho)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon\rho}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen.

Note that the second term in the summation is dominated by the first term as
\delta<=\rho
.
The worst-case number of samples is

\displaystyle O

\displaystyle\left\lparen s_{1/8}\log(1/\delta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}2^{2k}(K-k+1)t_{k}^{2}s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}(K-k+1)s_{t_{k}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil{\lg(1/\rho)}\right\rceil}(K-k+1)\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}2^{k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}2^{2k}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{2^{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{\sqrt{n}\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen.

∎

6.3.3
Closeness Testing

We use the general estimator of
Theorem

6.1
to get the first non-trivial bounds for replicable closeness testing. These bounds match our lower bound in
1.4
up to constant factors.

The statistic we will utilize is the
\chi^{2}
style statistic used in prior work on optimal non-replicable closeness testing
[
CDVV14
]
.
Unfortunately, this statistic (and any normalization of it) is not size-invariant, so we will utilize the general expectation-gap estimator in
Section

6.1
without in-expectation sampling bounds.

Lemma 8

(
[
CDVV14
]
)
.

Given a set
T
of Pois
(s)
samples from the product distribution
p\times q
over
[n]^{2}
, let
X_{i},Y_{i}
denote the number of occurrences of the
i
th domain elements in the samples from
p
and
q
, respectively. Define

Z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(X_{i}-Y_{i})^{2}-X_{i}-Y_{i}}{X_{i}+Y_{i}}.

(13)

We have

1.

\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}=s\sum_{i}\frac{(p_{i}-q_{i})^{2}}{p_{i}+q_{i}}\left(1-\frac{1-e^{-s(p_{i}+q_{i})}}{s(p_{i}+q_{i})}\right)
,

2.

\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}\geq\frac{s^{2}}{4n+2s}\|p-q\|_{1}^{2}
,

3.

If
p=q
then
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}=0
,

4.

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}\leq 2\min(n,s)+5m\sum_{i}\frac{(p_{i}-q_{i})^{2}}{p_{i}+q_{i}}\leq 10(\min(n,s)+s)
,

5.

If
s\geq n,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}\leq 10(n+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]})
.

Remark 3
.

To simplify the computations involved, much of the the analysis of distribution testing algorithms in the literature applies the standard “Poissonlization” trick
[
Can20
,
Can22
]
. In particular, this means we draw a random number of samples from a Poisson distribution rather than a fixed number. This simplifies the calculation as the number of occurrences of each element become mutually independent. Furthermore, it is without loss of generality using the fact that Poisson distributions are highly concentrated (in all cases, the failure probability of not receiving a sample that is within a constant factor of
\mathrm{Poi}(s)
for our choices of
s
can be made to be an arbitrary large polynomial in
\rho
). The same trick was also applied in the replicable uniformity testing paper of
[
LY24
]
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Consider
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\rho\leq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
C
be a constant with
\delta=\rho^{C}
.

Section

6.1
solves
(n,\varepsilon,\rho,\delta)
-replicable closeness testing and with worst-case sample complexity

O\left\lparen\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\rho^{2/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen.

Proof.

Consider the statistic
Z(s)
defined in
Equation

13
.
Using
Lemma

8
, we will choose the rest of the parameters of the expectation-gap statistic.
Let
\tau_{0}(s)=0
and
\tau_{1}(s)=\frac{s^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{4n+2s}
be the null and alternate thresholds.
Note that
\Delta(s)=\tau_{1}(s)
.
It remains to choose a variance bound
\sigma(s)
.
We will split into two cases depending on whether
s<n
(note that this is a property of the input parameters to the algorithm).

Case 1:

s<n
.
In this case,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq 20s
by
Lemma

8
, so it suffices to choose
\sigma(s)=\sqrt{20s}
.
The sampling breakpoints
s_{t}
must satisfy
f(s_{t})\leq t/2
.
Expanding this condition:

\displaystyle f(s_{t})\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{20s_{t}}}{\frac{s_{t}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{4n+2s_{t}}}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{20}\left\lparen 4n+2s_{t}\right\rparen}{s_{t}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{2}}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\frac{30n}{s_{t}^{3/2}\varepsilon^{2}}\leq t/2

(as
s<n
)

\displaystyle\iff s_{t}^{3/2}\geq\frac{60n}{\varepsilon^{2}t}

\displaystyle\iff s_{t}\geq\frac{16n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}t^{2/3}}.

Case 2:
s\geq n

Let
\|p-q\|_{1}=\alpha
.
In this case,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq 10\left\lparen n+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z}\right.}{Z}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z}\right]}\right\rparen
via
Lemma

8
.
We must choose
\sigma(s)
such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sigma(s)\left\lparen 1+\min\left\{0,\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)}\right\}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\iff\qquad

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\leq\sigma(s)\min\left\{1,\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\Delta(s)}\right\}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma(s)\geq\frac{\sqrt{10\left\lparen n+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\right\rparen}}{\min\left\{1,\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\Delta(s)}\right\}}

Note that the right hand side is maximized when
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\geq\Delta(s)
.
Recall from
Lemma

8
that
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\geq\frac{s^{2}\alpha^{2}}{4n+2s}
.
Therefore, it suffices to choose
\sigma(s)
with

\displaystyle\sigma(s)^{2}\geq\frac{10\left\lparen n+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\right\rparen}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}^{2}/\Delta(s)^{2}}=\frac{10n\Delta(s)^{2}}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}^{2}}+\frac{10\Delta(s)^{2}}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma(s)^{2}\geq 10n+10\Delta(s)=10n+\frac{10s^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{4n+2s}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\sigma(s)^{2}\geq 10n+5s\varepsilon^{2}.

Therefore, a valid choice is
\sigma(s)=\sqrt{10n+5s\varepsilon^{2}}
.
We will proceed by cases depending on which of these two terms dominates when bounding the sampling breakpoint
s_{t}
.

Assume first that
\sigma(s_{t})\leq\sqrt{20n}
.

\displaystyle f(s_{t})\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{20n}}{\frac{s_{t}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{4n+2s_{t}}}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{20n}(4n+2s_{t})}{s_{t}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\frac{27\sqrt{n}}{s_{t}\varepsilon^{2}}\leq t/2

(as
s\geq n
)

\displaystyle\iff s_{t}\geq\frac{54\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}t}.

Now, assume that
\sigma(s_{t})\leq\sqrt{10s_{t}\varepsilon^{2}}
.

\displaystyle f(s_{t})\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{10s_{t}\varepsilon^{2}}}{\frac{s_{t}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{4n+2s_{t}}}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{10}(4n+2s_{t})}{s_{t}^{3/2}\varepsilon}\leq t/2

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\frac{19}{\sqrt{s_{t}}\varepsilon}\leq t/2

(as
s\geq n
)

\displaystyle\iff\sqrt{s_{t}}\geq\frac{38}{\varepsilon t}

\displaystyle\iff s_{t}\geq\frac{1444}{\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}.

Completing the case analysis, across all parameter settings, the breakpoint
s_{t}
will be bounded by:

s_{t}=O\left\lparen\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}t^{2/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}\right\rparen.

Applying
Theorem

6.1
with
t=\rho
completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4
.

We are not aware of a size-invariant statistic for closeness testing which gets optimal bounds in the non-replicable setting.
Therefore, we do not get improved sampling bounds in expectation for this problem: this is an interesting open question.

7
Gaussian Mean Testing

In this section, we extend our study to continuous distributions by proving upper and lower bounds for Gaussian mean testing. In both cases, we make use of our frameworks for lower and upper bounds developed in the prior sections, but more work is needed to optimize these tools for the Gaussian setting.

We recall our upper bound for replicable Gaussian mean testing.

See
1.8

Our Algorithm.

We first present some intuition. At a high level, our algorithm works as follows. Since the distribution
\mathcal{D}
can be arbitrary in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we must be careful in filtering out pathological distributions. For example, imagine a distribution that samples from a standard gaussian with probability
1-\rho
, but a
\rho
-probability, picks points far away in a manner such that simple estimators such as the sample mean are tricked into thinking the mean is very large. This is evidently not replicable since our answer really hinges on an event with
\rho
-probability.

Thus, we first filter out “bad” distributions that do not behave like a standard identity-covariance Gaussian distribution, such as distributions which large “bias” in a certain direction. We capture this by filtering out distributions that have high probability of pairs of samples having large inner product (much larger than we expect for “well-behaved” Gaussians). We then use a variation of the standard Gaussian identity test, which computes the norm of the sum of data points, and accepts if the norm is below some threshold. For replicability, we modify this test to accept with some probability that depends on the norm, in a manner similar to the canonical replicable tester in
Section

4
.

Fix a parameter
L\geq 1
, that will be decided later. We start by making the following definition, which will be required to describe our algorithm in more detail. It helps us deal with the case of distributions that can sample points arbitrarily far away.

Definition 15
.

Given a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define
\mathcal{D}_{\proj}
represent the projected distribution of
\mathcal{D}
onto
\mathcal{B}
, the ball of radius
L\cdot\sqrt{d}
around the origin. Formally, we sample
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
and output
Y_{i}=\frac{X_{i}}{\max\left(1,\|X_{i}\|/(L\sqrt{d})\right)}
, meaning a point
X_{i}
in
\mathcal{B}
is left as is and a point
X_{i}
outside
\mathcal{B}
is projected to lie on the boundary of
\mathcal{B}
.

Based on this definition, we can assume that the distribution
\mathcal{D}
is contained in the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
with probability
1
, by replacing
\mathcal{D}
with
\mathcal{D}_{\proj}
if necessary. Formally, given
n
samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, we perform the algorithm on
\{Y_{i}\}
where
Y_{i}=\frac{X_{i}}{\max\left(\|X_{i}\|/(L\sqrt{d})\right)}
. Note that this preserves independence of the samples, so a replicable algorithm on
\{Y_{i}\}
is still replicable on
\{X_{i}\}
. However, note that our desired goal is now slightly different. The null hypothesis is now
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
, i.e., the projection of the standard Gaussian onto the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
, and the alternative hypothesis is
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
for any
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
. Even with this simplification, we still need to deal with the case that the distribution we sample from can be biased along certain directions.

Our algorithm as follows. For the sake of clarity, we break down our algorithm into three key primitives (denoted as Steps A, B, C below) and abstract away the contents of these steps to their own subsections (
Section

7.2
,
Section

7.3
,
Section

7.4
respectively).

Algorithm 7.1: Gaussian Mean Tester

1.

Let
s=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)
be sufficiently large, and set thresholds
T_{1},T_{2}
and
S
according to
Section

7.4
. We sample up to
5s
data points.

2.

Step A:
Sample
s
data points from
\mathcal{D}
, and run the replicable algorithm from
Section

7.2
that rejects with high probability if
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\geq 5T_{1}
.

3.

Step B:
Sample
2\cdot s
fresh data points. Given
2\cdot s
samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}
, consider creating a bipartite graph between
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
that connects
X_{i}
to
Y_{j}
if and only if
|\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle|\geq S
. Let
f(\mathcal{D})
be the maximum matching size in this bipartite graph.
Then, run the replicable algorithm from
Section

7.3
that rejects with high probability if
f(\mathcal{D})
exceeds
5T_{2}
.

4.

Step C:
Assuming we have not rejected in Steps
A
and
B
, draw
2s
fresh data points
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
8
8

8

In a slight abuse of notation, we again call these points
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
.
, and compute the value
\langle\sum_{i=1}^{s}X_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{s}Y_{i}\rangle
. Use the replicable Expectation-Gap
Section

6.1
(detailed in
Section

7.4
) to reject if this value is too large, and accept if this value is small enough.

7.1
Threshold Algorithm

We note the following simple thresholding algorithm which will be used for Steps A and B. Its guarantees are very similar to that of our Expectation-Gap Estimator framework of
Definition

12
and
Theorem

6.1
in
Section

6
. However, we find it easier to work with the slightly modified guarantees that deal with high probability events rather than directly dealing with variances.

The threshold algorithm is as follows: Given a threshold parameter
T
, and a dataset
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, suppose that
h:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a positive-valued statistic (deterministic in
X
). We consider the following algorithm, that we call
\mathcal{A}_{h,T}
: compute
\alpha=\frac{3T-h(X)}{T}
, sample
r\sim\Unif([0,1])
, and accept if and only if
\alpha\leq r
. Note that if
h
can be efficiently computed, then the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{h,T}
can be as well.

We have the following analysis of this basic threshold algorithm. Since this is a slight modification of the proof of
Theorem

6.1
, its proof is presented in
Appendix

A
.

Proposition 4
.

Fix a function
h:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and parameters
T\geq 0
and
0<\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
. Suppose that for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{X}
and i.i.d. samples
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\}\sim\mathcal{D}
, there exists a value
q=q(\mathcal{D})
(which may implicitly depend on
h
and
T
) such that with probability
1-\delta
over the randomness of
X
,
|h(X)-q|\leq\rho\cdot\max(|q|,T)
. Then, the following claims hold:

•

\mathcal{A}_{h,T}
is
12\rho
-replicable.

•

If
q(\mathcal{D})\geq 5T
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the algorithm rejects.

•

If
q(\mathcal{D})\leq T
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the algorithm accepts.

For Step C, we directly rely on
Theorem

6.1
.

7.2
Step A

First, we note the following basic consequence of the Matrix Chernoff bound
[
T+15
]
.

Lemma 9
.

Fix any parameters
L\geq 1
,
\delta\leq 1
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that each sample
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
is bounded in
\ell_{2}
norm by
L\sqrt{d}
with probability
1
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the operator norm of the empirical covariance,
\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op},
is in the range
\left[\|\Sigma\|_{op}-H,\|\Sigma\|_{op}+H\right]
, where

H=O\left(\max\left(\frac{d}{s}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\delta},\sqrt{\frac{d}{s}\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\delta}}\right)\right).

Proof.

Note that
\|X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\|_{op}\leq L^{2}\cdot d
for all
X_{i}
. Therefore, by Matrix Chernoff, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right.}{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right]}\leq d\cdot e^{-O(\min(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}\cdot s/(L^{2}d))}.

(14)

Next, let
v
be a unit vector such that
v^{\top}\Sigma v=\|\Sigma\|_{op}
. If we consider
v^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right)v=\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}\langle v,X_{i}\rangle^{2},
note that each
\langle v,X_{i}\rangle^{2}
is an independent random variable with mean
\|\Sigma\|_{op}
and is bounded by
L^{2}d
since we assume
\|X_{i}\|\leq L\sqrt{d}
with probability
1
. So, by a standard Chernoff bound,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right.}{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right\|_{op}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right]}\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\frac{1}{s}\langle v,X_{i}\rangle^{2}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right.}{\frac{1}{s}\langle v,X_{i}\rangle^{2}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\frac{1}{s}\langle v,X_{i}\rangle^{2}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\right]}\leq e^{-O(\min(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}\cdot s/(L^{2}d))}.

(15)

Hence, if we set
\varepsilon
to be a sufficiently large multiple of
\max\left(\frac{d}{s\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\delta},\sqrt{\frac{d}{s\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\delta}}\right),
both (
14
) and (
15
) are at most
\delta/2
. By writing
H=\varepsilon\cdot\|\Sigma\|_{op},
the lemma is complete.
∎

Lemma 10
.

Let
\delta\leq\rho\leq 0.01
. There exists a
O(\rho)
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with the following properties. Fix a parameter
L\geq 1
, and let
T_{1}
be any parameter such that
T_{1}\geq O\left(\frac{d}{s\cdot\rho^{2}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\delta}\right)
. Then, for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
contained in the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
around the origin:

•

if
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\leq T_{1},
the algorithm, given
s
samples from
\mathcal{D}
, accepts with probability at least
1-\delta
.

•

if
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\geq 5T_{1},
the algorithm, given
s
samples from
\mathcal{D}
, rejects with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Given data points
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}
, let
t
be the statistic
\|\frac{1}{s}\sum X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\|_{op}
. We show that for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
,
t=\|\frac{1}{s}\sum X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\|_{op}
lies in the interval
[q-\rho\cdot\max(q,T_{1}),q+\rho\cdot\max(q,T_{1})]
, where
q=\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}
, with probability
1-\delta
over the randomness of
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
.
To see why, by Lemma
9
, we know that
t
lies in the interval
[q-H,q+H]
with
1-\delta
probability, where
H\leq O\left(\max\left(\frac{d}{s}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho},\sqrt{\frac{d}{s}\cdot q\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}}\right)\right)
. So, it suffices to verify that
H\leq\rho\cdot\max(q,T_{1})
. If
q\leq T_{1}
, then it suffices to verify that
\rho\cdot T_{1}\geq O\left(\max\left(\frac{d}{s}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho},\sqrt{\frac{d}{s}\cdot T_{1}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}}\right)\right)
. This holds as long as
T_{1}\geq O\left(\frac{d}{s\cdot\rho^{2}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}\right)
. If
q\geq T_{1}
, when we need to verify that
\rho\cdot q\geq O\left(\max\left(\frac{d}{s}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho},\sqrt{\frac{d}{s}\cdot q\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}}\right)\right)
. This holds as long as
q\geq O\left(\frac{d}{s\cdot\rho^{2}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}\right)
, which is true if
T_{1}\geq O\left(\frac{d}{s\cdot\rho^{2}}\cdot L^{2}\cdot\log\frac{d}{\rho}\right)
since we assumed
q\geq T_{1}
.

Therefore, by Proposition
4
, the algorithm that computes
\mathcal{A}_{h,T_{1}}
where
h(X)=\|\frac{1}{s}\sum X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\|_{op}
is
O(\rho)
-replicable. Moreover, since
q=\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}
, the accuracy guarantees of the lemma hold as well.
∎

7.3
Step B

In this section, we consider the following bipartite graph on data points.

Definition 16
.

For any data points
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
and a threshold parameter
S\geq 0
, define the bipartite graph
G_{S}(X,Y)
on
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\},

Y=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\}
that connects
X_{i},Y_{j}
if
|\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle|\geq S
. Define
M_{S}(X,Y)
to be the maximum matching size between
X,Y
in this bipartite graph
G_{S}(X,Y)
.

Our first step in this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 11
.

Fix
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\}
and
S
, and let
Y=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\}
be drawn i.i.d. from any distribution
\mathcal{D}
. Then, the random variable
M_{S}(X,Y)
, as a function of
Y
, satisfies the concentration inequality

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\right.}{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\right]}\leq 2\cdot\exp\left(-0.1\cdot\min\left(t,\frac{t^{2}}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}}\right)\right).

To prove this lemma, we use
Theorem

3.2
from
[
BLM00
]
(see
Section

3
).

Proof of
Lemma

11
.

We apply
Theorem

3.2
as follows. Let
f(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s})
be the maximum matching size
M_{S}(X,Y)
, and let
g(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{i-1},Y_{i+1},\dots,Y_{s})
be the maximum matching size between
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{i-1},Y_{i+1},\dots,Y_{s}
. Adding a data point to
Y
will never decrease the matching size and will increase it by at most
1
. Moreover, if there is a maximum matching between
X
and
Y
of some size
k
, using
Y_{i_{1}},\dots,Y_{i_{k}}
, then removing
x_{i}
for
i\not\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
will maintain the maximum matching size at
k
. So,
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})-g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{s})
is positive for at most
k
choices of
i
, and is at most
1
in that setting. Thus,
f
satisfies the required properties.

Hence, for any fixed
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},S
, by
Theorem

3.2
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\right.}{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}\right|\geq t}\right]}\leq 2\cdot\exp\left(-0.1\cdot\min\left(t,\frac{t^{2}}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}}\right)\right).

as desired.
∎

Lemma 12
.

For any distribution
\mathcal{D}
and any fixed threshold
S\geq 0
, there exists a value
\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}(\mathcal{D},S,\delta)
such that for samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}
, the matching size
M_{S}(X,Y)
satisfies
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mu_{1}|}\right.}{|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mu_{1}|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|M_{S}(X,Y)-\mu_{1}|}\right]}\leq\rho\cdot\max\left(\mu_{1},O\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Suppose we sample
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\},

X^{\prime}=\{X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime}\},

Y=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\}
, and
Y^{\prime}=\{Y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,Y_{s}^{\prime}\}
, all i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}
. Define
\mu_{1}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}
, where
X
is fixed. By Lemma
11
, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
, both
M_{S}(X,Y)
and
M_{S}(X,Y^{\prime})
are within
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\sqrt{\mu_{1}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}})
of
\mu_{1}
. Next, define
\mu_{2}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y^{\prime})}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y^{\prime})}\right]}
. Again, applying Lemma
11
, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
, both
M_{S}(X,Y^{\prime})
and
M_{S}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
are within
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\sqrt{\mu_{2}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}})
of
\mu_{2}
. So, by Triangle inequality, with probability at least
1-\delta,
over the randomness of
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
both
M_{S}(X,Y)
and
M_{S}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
are within
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\sqrt{\mu_{1}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}})
of
\mu_{1}:=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right.}{M_{S}(X,Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{M_{S}(X,Y)}\right]}
.

Therefore, there exists a choice of
\mu_{1}
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
over
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
,
|M_{S}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})-\mu_{1}|\leq O\left(\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\sqrt{\mu_{1}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)\leq\rho\cdot\max\left(\mu_{1},O\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
.
∎

Hence, we can apply
Proposition

4
again, to obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 7.1
.

Let
\delta\leq\rho\leq 0.01
, and let
S\geq 0
by any threshold. For
\mu_{1}(\mathcal{D},S,\delta)
as in
Lemma

12
, and for some threshold
T_{2}=O\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
, there exists an
O(\rho)
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}
that accepts with probability
1-\delta
whenever
\mu_{1}(\mathcal{D},S,\delta)\leq T_{2}
and rejects with probability
1-\delta
whenever
\mu_{1}(\mathcal{D},S,\delta)\geq 5T_{2}
.

7.4
Step C

Setting of parameters.

We now set parameters to properly initialize our
Section

7
. Let
K
be a sufficiently large polylogarithmic multiple of
s,d,\frac{1}{\alpha},\frac{1}{\rho}
.
Fix parameters
L=K
,
S=K\cdot\sqrt{d}
,
T_{1}=\left(1+\frac{d}{s\cdot\rho^{2}}\right)\cdot L^{2}\cdot K
, and
T_{2}=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\cdot K
. Now, we define
\mathcal{D}
contained in the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
to be a
good
distribution, if
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\leq 5T_{1}
, and
\mu_{1}(\mathcal{D},S,\frac{\rho}{L^{4}d^{2}})\leq 5T_{2}
, where
\mu_{1}
is defined in
Lemma

12
.

Now suppose we are given samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is good, and we compute the statistic
\langle X_{1}+\cdots+X_{s},Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{s}\rangle
. In expectation, this statistic equals
s^{2}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2},
where
\mu=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}}\right.}{X_{i}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}}\right]}
. The variance of this statistic is

\displaystyle O\left(\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle}\right.}{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle}\right]}+s^{3}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{X_{1},Y_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\sim\mathcal{D}}(\langle X_{1},Y_{1}\rangle,\langle X_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)\right)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right]}+s^{3}\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{1},Y_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\langle X_{1},Y_{1}\rangle\langle X_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}\right.}{\langle X_{1},Y_{1}\rangle\langle X_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\langle X_{1},Y_{1}\rangle\langle X_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}\rangle}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right]}+s^{3}\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{1}\sim\mathcal{D}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\right]}\right).

To bound
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\langle X_{1},\mu\rangle^{2}}\right]}
, note that we can write this as
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mu^{\top}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}\mu}\right.}{\mu^{\top}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}\mu}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mu^{\top}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}\mu}\right]}=\mu^{\top}\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\right]}\cdot\mu\leq\|\mu\|^{2}\cdot\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\leq 5T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}.

To bound
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right]},
we again consider the matching size
M_{S}(X,Y)
from the previous subsection. If the matching has size
m
, there are subsets
A,B\subset[n]
of size
m
, such that for all
i\not\in A,j\not\in B,

|\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle|\leq S
.
We write

\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}=\sum_{i\not\in A,j\not\in B}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i\in A}X_{i}^{\top}\cdot\left(\sum_{j}Y_{j}Y_{j}^{\top}\right)\cdot X_{i}+\sum_{j\in A}Y_{j}^{\top}\cdot\left(\sum_{j}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\right)\cdot Y_{j}-\sum_{i\in A,j\in B}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}.

The first term is at most
s^{2}\cdot S^{2},
since
|\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle|\leq S
if
i\not\in A,j\not\in B
. The second term is at most
m\cdot\|X_{i}\|^{2}\cdot\|\sum_{j}Y_{j}Y_{j}^{\top}\|_{op}
. By
Lemma

9
, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{L^{4}d^{2}},

\|\sum_{j}Y_{j}Y_{j}^{\top}\|_{op}=s\cdot\|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{j}Y_{j}Y_{j}^{\top}\|_{op}\leq O(s\cdot T_{1})
. The third term can be bounded similarly, and the fourth term is at most
0
. Overall, with probability at least
1-O(\frac{1}{L^{4}d^{2}})
,
\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}\leq O(s^{2}\cdot S^{2}+T_{2}\cdot L^{2}d\cdot s\cdot T_{1})
, and otherwise, it is still bounded by
s^{2}\cdot(L\sqrt{d})^{4}=s^{2}\cdot L^{4}d^{2}
, since there are
s
choices for each of
i,j
and
\|X_{i}\|,\|Y_{j}\|\leq L\sqrt{d}
. So,

\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right.}{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\sum_{i,j}\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle^{2}}\right]}\leq O(s^{2}\cdot S^{2}+T_{2}\cdot L^{2}d\cdot s\cdot T_{1}).

Overall, the mean of the statistic is
s^{2}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}
, and the variance is bounded by
O(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}+s^{2}\cdot S^{2}+L^{2}\cdot sd\cdot T_{1}\cdot T_{2})
.

Our approach, based on this calculation, is the following. First, we show that the projected null hypothesis (i.e.,
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
) is good. Then, we show that for good distributions, we can create an algorithm that is replicable on good distributions, accepts
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
, and rejects
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
whenever
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
is good and
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
. Finally, by combining with the previous subsections, we can extend both the replicability and accuracy guarantees beyond good distributions.

Lemma 13
.

We have
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}\|_{op}\leq 1
.
Also, if
X=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\},Y=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
, then with probability at least
1-\frac{\rho}{L^{4}d^{2}}
, the maximum matching
M_{S}(X,Y)
, as defined in
Definition

16
has size
0
. Hence,
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
is good.

Proof.

By symmetry,
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
has mean
0
and covariance that is a scalar multiple of identity. Also, if
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and
\hat{x}
is the projection,
\|x\|\geq\|\hat{x}\|
, so
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|\hat{x}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|\hat{x}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|\hat{x}\|^{2}}\right]}\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|x\|^{2}}\right.}{\|x\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|x\|^{2}}\right]}\leq d
. So, the trace of the covariance matrix is
d
, which means the operator norm of
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right.}{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}\right]}
is at most
1
.

Given
X_{i},Y_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
, the probability that
|\langle X_{i},Y_{j}\rangle|\geq K\sqrt{d}
is at most
e^{-\Omega(K)}
. So, assuming
K\geq\poly\log(s,d,1/\rho),
this probability is at most
\frac{\rho}{L^{4}d^{2}s^{2}}
. By taking a union bound over
s^{2}
pairs
(X_{i},Y_{j})
, we have that the corresponding graph
G_{S}(X,Y)
is in fact empty with at least
\frac{\rho}{L^{4}d^{2}}
probability.
∎

We will need the following auxiliary proposition, which characterizes the norm of a spherical Gaussian after the projection.

Proposition 5
.

For a vector
\mu
and any
\alpha\leq 1
, consider the mean of the distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj},
i.e., where we sample
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
and project on to the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
. If
\mu
is the origin, then the mean of the distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
is also the origin, and if
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
, then the mean of the distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
has norm at least
\alpha/2
.

Proof.

The claim when
\mu
is the origin is trivial by symmetry.

First, assume
\alpha\leq\|\mu\|\leq L\sqrt{d}/2
. Consider sampling
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
and
Y_{1}
as the projection of
X_{1}
. Note that
\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|=\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|\cdot\mathbb{I}[X_{1}\neq Y_{1}]
, since if
X_{1}=Y_{1}
then
\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|=0
. So,
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\right]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}[X_{1}\neq Y_{1}]}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}[X_{1}\neq Y_{1}]}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}[X_{1}\neq Y_{1}]}\right]}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X_{1}\neq Y_{1}}\right.}{X_{1}\neq Y_{1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X_{1}\neq Y_{1}}\right]}}
, by Cauchy-Schwarz. The probability that
X_{1}\neq Y_{1}
equals the probability that
\|X\|\geq L\sqrt{d}
, which for
\|\mu\|\leq L\sqrt{d}/2
and
L
at least a sufficiently large constant, is at most
e^{-L}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}\leq 2\cdot(\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}+\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]})
, and we know
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}=\|\mu\|^{2}+d
and
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}\leq L^{2}d
since
Y
is always contained in the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
. Overall, this means
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}\leq 2\cdot(L^{2}d+(L\sqrt{d}/2)^{2}+d)\leq 4L^{2}d
, which means
\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|^{2}}\right]}\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X\neq Y}\right.}{X\neq Y}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X\neq Y}\right]}}\leq 2e^{-L/2}\cdot L\sqrt{d}.
Assuming
L
is a sufficiently large polylogarithmic multiple of
1/\alpha
and
d
, this is at most
\alpha/2
. Hence,
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\right.}{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\|X_{1}-Y_{1}\|}\right]}\leq\alpha/2
, and since
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{X}\right.}{X}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{X}\right]}=\mu
which has norm at least
\alpha
, by the Triangle inequality
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Y_{1}}\right.}{Y_{1}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Y_{1}}\right]}\|\geq\alpha/2
.

Alternatively, suppose
\|\mu\|\geq L\sqrt{d}/2
. In that case, let
\hat{\mu}
be the projection of
\mu
onto the ball of radius
L\sqrt{d}
. Since the projection never dilates distances, for any point
x
with projection
\hat{x},

\|\hat{\mu}-\hat{x}\|\leq\|\mu-x\|
. So, for
x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
,
\|\hat{\mu}-\hat{x}\|\leq\|\mu-x\|\leq\sqrt{\|\mu-x\|^{2}}=\sqrt{d},
which means that by Triangle inequality,
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\hat{x}}\right.}{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\hat{x}}\right]}\|\geq\|\hat{\mu}\|-\sqrt{d}
. Since
\|\hat{\mu}\|=\min(L\sqrt{d},\|\mu\|)\geq L\sqrt{d}/2
, we have
\|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\hat{x}}\right.}{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\hat{x}}\right]}\|\geq\sqrt{d}\geq\alpha
.
∎

We are now ready to show that there is a replicable algorithm, at least for good distributions, that can distinguish between
\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
with
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
.

Lemma 14
.

Suppose
s\geq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)
, and let
K,L,S,T_{1},T_{2}
be as in the beginning of this subsection.
There exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}
with the following properties.

•

For any good distribution
\mathcal{D}
, if given samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}
and
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime},Y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,Y_{s}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}
, we have

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s};r)=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s};r)=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1},\dots,X_{s},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s};r)=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}^{\prime};r)}\right]}\geq 1-\rho
.

•

The algorithm accepts
2s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
with probability at least
0.99
.

•

For any
\mu
with
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
, if
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
is good, the algorithm rejects
2s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
with probability at least
0.99
.

Proof.

Consider sampling
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
,
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
from
\mathcal{D}
, and compute the statistic
Z=\langle\sum X_{i},\sum Y_{j}\rangle
. For any distribution
\mathcal{D}
satisfying the assumptions in the lemma statement, the expectation of
Z
is
s^{2}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}
and the variance is
O(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}+s^{2}\cdot S^{2}+L^{2}\cdot nd\cdot T_{1}T_{2})
. We upper bound the first term in the variance as

s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{2}\leq O\left(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\left(\frac{\|\mu\|^{4}}{\alpha^{2}}+\alpha^{2}\right)\right),

and instead use the variance upper bound of
O(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{4}/\alpha^{2}+s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\alpha^{2}+s^{2}\cdot S^{2}+L^{2}\cdot sd\cdot T_{1}T_{2})
.

Our goal is to use
Theorem

6.1
to prove the lemma. Towards that end, let
\tau_{0}(s)=0,\tau_{1}(s)=s^{2}\alpha^{2}/4
be the null and alternate hypothesis thresholds. Note that
\Delta(s)=\tau_{1}(s)
and

\left\lparen 1+\max\left\{0,\frac{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}-\tau_{1}(s)}{\Delta(s)},\frac{\tau_{0}(s)-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}{\Delta(s)}\right\}\right\rparen=O\left(1+\frac{\|\mu\|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right).

It remains to pick an appropriate function
\sigma(s)
, which we do based on which terms in the variance of
Z
dominate.

Case 1:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq O(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\|\mu\|^{4}/\alpha^{2})
. In this case, it suffices to choose
\sigma(s)=\Omega(\alpha s^{1.5}\sqrt{T_{1}})
, since this gives

\frac{\|\mu\|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\cdot\alpha s^{1.5}\sqrt{T_{1}}\geq\Omega\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}}\right).

The sampling breakpoints
s_{t}
must satisfy
f(s_{t})\leq t/2
. Expanding this condition, and recalling that
\Delta(s)=\Theta(s^{2}\alpha^{2})
, it suffices to pick
t
such that

t\geq\Omega\left(\sqrt{\frac{T_{1}}{\alpha^{2}s_{t}}}\right),

or in other words, recalling our setting of
T_{1}
,

s_{t}\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}t^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho\alpha t}\right).

Case 2:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq O(s^{3}\cdot T_{1}\cdot\alpha^{2})
. It suffices to pick
\sigma(s)=\Omega(s^{1.5}\alpha\sqrt{T_{1}})
and following a similar reasoning as above, we arrive at the same lower bound of
s_{t}
as Case 1.

Case 3:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq O(s^{2}\cdot S^{2})
. It suffices to pick
\sigma(s)=\Omega(sS)
and following the same reasoning as in Case 1, it suffices to pick
t
such that

s_{t}\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{t\alpha^{2}}\right).

Case 4:

\mathrm{\mathbf{Var}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{Z(s)}\right.}{Z(s)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{Z(s)}\right]}\leq O(L^{2}\cdot sd\cdot T_{1}T_{2})
. It suffices to pick
\sigma(s)=\Omega(L\sqrt{sdT_{1}T_{2}})
and again the same reasoning implies that it suffices to pick
t
such that

s_{t}^{3}\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{dT_{1}T_{2}}{t^{2}\alpha^{4}}\right).

We now simplify the above expression. Recalling our values of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, we have
dT_{1}T_{2}\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\rho^{2}}+\frac{d^{2}}{s_{t}\rho^{4}}\right)
, and so it suffices to pick
s_{t}
and
t
such that

s_{t}\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d^{1/3}}{\rho^{2/3}t^{2/3}\alpha^{4/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho\alpha\sqrt{t}}\right).

Completing the case analysis, across all parameter settings, the breakpoint
s_{t}
will be bounded by

s_{t}=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}t^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho\alpha t}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{t\alpha^{2}}+\frac{d^{1/3}}{\rho^{2/3}t^{2/3}\alpha^{4/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho\alpha\sqrt{t}}\right).

Applying
Theorem

6.1
with
t=\rho
and using the fact that

\frac{1}{\rho\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}\alpha}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{4/3}\alpha^{4/3}}\right),

gives us a sample complexity of

\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha\rho^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho\alpha^{2}}\right),

meaning that as long as
s\geq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)
, we have an
O(\rho)
-replicable algorithm (at least, a replicable algorithm on distributions
\mathcal{D}
satisfying the assumption), that accepts w.h.p. on any good distribution with mean
0
and rejects w.h.p. on any good distribution with mean at least
\alpha/2
in absolute value.

By
Lemma

13
and
Proposition

5
,
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
is good and has mean
0
, which means that the algorithm accepts w.h.p. Also, by
Proposition

5
, if
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
, then
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
has mean at least
\alpha/2
. So, either
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
is not good or the algorithm rejects w.h.p. This completes the lemma.
∎

Putting things together.

To summarize, our overall algorithm is to set the parameters
L,S,T_{1},T_{2}
as in the beginning of
Section

7.4
, set
s=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}\rho}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha\rho^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)
, and then run
\mathcal{A}_{1}
from
Lemma

10
with
s
samples,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
from
Corollary

7.1
with
s
fresh samples, and
\mathcal{A}_{3}
from
Lemma

14
with
2s
fresh samples. If the distribution
\mathcal{D}
is bad, then either
\mathcal{A}_{1}
or
\mathcal{A}_{2}
will reject with
1-\rho
probability, which means we also have
O(\rho)
-replicability. If
\mathcal{D}
is good, all of
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}
are
O(\rho)
-replicable, so if we run them with fresh samples and fresh randomness, we still have
O(\rho)
-replicability. Finally, if given samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,I)_{\proj}
, we pass all three steps with high probability, and if given samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)_{\proj}
, we fail at least one of the three steps with high probability. Hence, this completes the proof of
1.8
.

7.5
Lower Bound

We recall our lower bound on replicable Gaussian mean testing.

See
1.9

We start by proving a generalization of
Lemma

3
that allows us to convert replicable algorithms into (canonical) replicable algorithms that only depend on the
sufficient statistic
of the data, assuming the samples are drawn from a parameterized distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta
. However, this will come at the cost of the new algorithm being only
weakly
replicable (recall the definition of weakly replicable in
Definition

4
).
In our application, the parameterized distributions are
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
for any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e.,
\theta=\mu
and
\Theta=\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

Lemma 15

(Sufficient Statistic Invariant Algorithm)
.

Given a parameterized distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
and regions
\Theta_{{accept}},\Theta_{{reject}}
, suppose
\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)
is a
\rho
-replicable algorithm that distinguishes between distributions
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{{accept}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{{reject}}
. Then, there exists a weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}(S(X);r)
that solves the same problem on
s
samples with the same accuracy and only depends on the sufficient statistic
S(X)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has the canonical property, meaning
r\sim\Unif[0,1]
and there exists a deterministic function
q(S(X))\in[0,1]
where
\mathcal{A}_{2}(S(X);r)=1
if
r\leq q(S(X))
and
0
otherwise.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)
be the algorithm defined in Lemma
1
with the deterministic function
f:\mathcal{X}^{s}\rightarrow[0,1]
.
Choose an arbitrary
\theta
, and consider the following deterministic function of the sample set
X
,
q:S(\mathcal{X}^{s})\rightarrow[0,1]\,
:

q(S(X))\coloneqq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}\,.

(16)

(Recall that
Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)
means we generate
Y=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s})\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
conditional on
S(Y)=S(X)
.)
Note that by definition of sufficient statistic, the choice of
{\theta}
does not affect the conditional distribution
Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)
.
Moreover, the right-hand side of (
16
) only depends on
X
through
S(X)
, so
q
can be defined.
The algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)
operates similarly to
\mathcal{A}_{1}(X;r)
, except that it uses
q
instead of
f
. For a random seed
r\sim\Unif([0,1])
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)
outputs
accept
if
r\leq q(S(X))
, and
reject
otherwise.

To check the accuracy of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, first consider any
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
, where
\theta\in\Theta_{{accept}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(S(X))}\right.}{q(S(X))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(S(X))}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{0}(Y;r)=\text{{accept} }}\right]}\,.

(Using Lemma
1
, Eq. (
1
))

The third line holds since if we sample
X\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}
and
Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X),
it is equivalent to sample
S(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s})
where
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta},
and then
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
have the right conditional distibution given
S(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s})
. So,
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s}
in fact have the same marginal distribution as
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}
. The same argument holds for
\theta\in\Theta_{\text{reject}}
, so
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has the same probabilities of outputting
accept
and
reject
as
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and thus inherits the accuracy guarantees of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Next, we show weak replicability of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. For any distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
, similar to Lemma
1
, we have:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}&=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right]}\\
&=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}\,,\end{split}

(17)

where in the last line, we use the structure of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Using the definition of
q
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q(S(X))\penalty\ -\penalty\ q(S(X^{\prime}))}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\right.}{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y)}\right.}{f(Y)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y)}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X),Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}|}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X),Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y)=S(X),Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}|S(Y^{\prime})=S(X^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right.}{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|f(Y)-f(Y^{\prime})}\right]}|}\right]}

(Via triangle inequality)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{|f(X)-f(Y)|}\right.}{|f(X)-f(Y)|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{|f(X)-f(Y)|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{\otimes s},r\sim\Unif{[0,1]}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{1}(Y^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\rho.

(Since
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is
\rho
-replicable)

Hence, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, we show that in the Gaussian mean testing setting, the algorithm can be assumed to only depend on the Euclidean norm of the empirical mean of the samples.

Lemma 16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}(X;r)
be a
\rho
-replicable algorithm that distinguishes between
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I):\mu=0
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I):\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
. Then, there exists another weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|,r)
, ony depending on
X
through the norm of its empirical mean
\|\bar{X}\|=\left\|\frac{X_{1}+\cdots+X_{s}}{s}\right\|
, that also distinguishes between
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I):\mu=0
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I):\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has the canonical form, meaning
r\sim\Unif[0,1]
and there exists a deterministic function
q_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|)\in[0,1]
where
\mathcal{A}_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|;r)=1
if
r\leq q_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|)
and
0
otherwise.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2
,
\bar{X}=\frac{X_{1}+\cdots+X_{s}}{s}
is a sufficient statistic for the parameterized distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
. By
Lemma

15
, we can start with the weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\bar{X};r)
, which distinguishes between
