\mu=0
and
\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
by sampling
r\sim\Unif[0,1]
and outputting
1
if
r\leq q(\bar{X})
, for some deterministic function
q
.

Let
O_{d}
represent the uniform (Haar) measure over
d\times d
orthogonal matrices. Next, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
, and let
q_{3}=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}
. Then,
H(\bar{X})
is a random vector on the sphere of radius
\|\bar{X}\|
, so
q_{3}
only depends on
\|\bar{X}\|
. So, for any
X_{1},\dots,X_{s},
we can define
q_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|)=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}
.

First, we check that the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|;r)
, which outputs
1
if
r\leq q_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|)
and
0
otherwise, is accurate. If
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I),
then
\bar{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\frac{I}{s})
. So,
H(\bar{X})
has the distribution of
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\frac{I}{s})
followed by a random rotation. This is the same as first randomly rotating
\mu
to get some
\mu^{\prime}
with
\|\mu^{\prime}\|=\|\mu\|
, and then sampling from
\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},\frac{I}{s})
. So, if
\beta=\|\mu\|,
then

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s},H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{\mu^{\prime}:\|\mu^{\prime}\|=\beta}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right.}{q(\bar{X})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right.}{q(\bar{X})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right.}{q(\bar{X})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right]}}\right]}.

If
\mu=0
, then
\mu^{\prime}=0
with probability
1
, and
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right.}{q(\bar{X})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right]}\geq 1-\delta
. Thus,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right]}\geq 1-\delta
as well. Alternatively, if
\beta=\|\mu\|\geq\alpha
, then
\|\mu^{\prime}\|\geq\alpha
with probability
1
, and
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right.}{q(\bar{X})}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(\bar{X})}\right]}\leq\delta
. Thus,
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)={accept}}\right]}\leq\delta
as well. Hence, the same accuracy bounds hold.

To prove weak replicability, suppose that
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|{q_{3}(\|X\|)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q_{3}(\|X^{\prime}\|)}\right|}\right.}{\left|{q_{3}(\|X\|)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q_{3}(\|X^{\prime}\|)}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|{q_{3}(\|X\|)\penalty\ -\penalty\ q_{3}(\|X^{\prime}\|)}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right]}\right|}\right.}{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right]}\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X}))}\right]}-\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right.}{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))}\right]}\right|}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime},H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}

(Via triangle inequality)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\right]}.

If you fix
H
, then if
X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}
, then
H(\bar{X})
has the same distribution as the empirical mean of
s
samples drawn from
\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)
, by rotational symmetry of the Gaussian. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right.}{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(H(\bar{X}))-q(H(\bar{X^{\prime}}))\right|}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right.}{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right.}{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right.}{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\left|q(\bar{X})-q(\bar{X^{\prime}})\right|}\right]}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s},r\sim\Unif{[0,1]}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right.}{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s},r\sim\Unif{[0,1]}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(H(\mu),I)^{\otimes s},r\sim\Unif{[0,1]}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X;r)\neq\mathcal{A}_{2}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}}\right]}.

(Definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
q
)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim O_{d}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{1-\rho}\right.}{1-\rho}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{1-\rho}\right]}=1-\rho

(Weak replicability of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
)

Therefore, the overall probability
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{r,X,X^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)^{\otimes s}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{3}(X;r)\not=\mathcal{A}_{3}(X^{\prime};r)}\right]}
is at least
1-\rho
. Hence,
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is also
\rho
-weakly replicable.
∎

We need the following auxiliary lemma about total variation distance between norms of Gaussians.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 0
, and
d,s\in\mathbb{N}
be positive integers. Let
Z_{1}
be the distribution in which we pick an arbitrary
\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of norm
\beta_{1}
, sample
z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\frac{I}{s})
, and
Z_{1}=\|z_{1}\|
. (By rotational symmetry of Gaussians, note that the choice of
\mu_{1}
doesn’t affect the distribution of
Z_{1}
.) Likewise, let
Z_{2}
be the distribution in which we pick an arbitrary
\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of norm
\beta_{2}
, sample
z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\frac{I}{s})
, and
Z_{2}=\|z_{2}\|
.

Then, if
s\leq\max\left(\frac{c}{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}},\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}\right)
, then
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1},Z_{2})\leq 0.5
.

Proof.

It is equivalent to look at
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1}^{2},Z_{2}^{2})
since
Z_{1},Z_{2}\geq 0
always. Moreover, we may assume WLOG that
\mu_{1}=(\beta_{1},0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu_{2}=(\beta_{2},0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Then,
Z_{1}^{2}=(\beta_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+(\frac{z_{2}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\cdots+(\frac{z_{d}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}
, and
Z_{2}^{2}=(\beta_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+(\frac{z_{2}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\cdots+(\frac{z_{d}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}
, where
z_{1},\dots,z_{d}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

First, if
\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\leq\frac{0.1}{\sqrt{s}}
, then the total variation distance between
\beta_{1}+\frac{z}{\sqrt{s}}
and
\beta_{2}+\frac{z}{\sqrt{s}}
is at most
0.5
if
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Hence, the total variation distance between
(\beta_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}
and
(\beta_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}
is also at most
0.5
. Hence, we can couple the values
z_{2},\dots,z_{d}
, to obtain that
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1}^{2},Z_{2}^{2})\leq 0.5
, as long as
\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\leq\frac{0.1}{\sqrt{s}}
, or equivalently, if
s\leq\frac{0.01}{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}}
.

For any
d\geq 2
, note that
(\frac{z_{2}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\cdots+(\frac{z_{d}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}=\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}
. By coupling
z_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1}^{2},Z_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{d_{\textit{TV}}\left((\beta_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\right.}{d_{\textit{TV}}\left((\beta_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{d_{\textit{TV}}\left((\beta_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}})^{2}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})+(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\cdot\frac{2z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\right.}{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})+(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\cdot\frac{2z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})+(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\cdot\frac{2z_{1}}{\sqrt{s}}+\frac{1}{s}\cdot\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\underbrace{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})\cdot s+2(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\sqrt{s}\cdot z_{1}+\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}_{T}}\right.}{\underbrace{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})\cdot s+2(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\sqrt{s}\cdot z_{1}+\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}_{T}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\underbrace{d_{\textit{TV}}\left(\chi_{d-1}^{2},(\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2})\cdot s+2(\beta_{2}-\beta_{1})\sqrt{s}\cdot z_{1}+\chi_{d-1}^{2}\right)}_{T}}\right]}.

By
Proposition

1
, as long as
|\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2}|\cdot s+2|\beta_{2}-\beta_{1}|\cdot\sqrt{s}\cdot|z_{1}|\leq 0.001\sqrt{d-1}
, the expression
T
is at most
0.1
. For any positive
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
,
|\beta_{2}-\beta_{1}|\leq\sqrt{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}
Hence, as long as
|z_{1}|\leq 2
and
s\leq\frac{c\cdot\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}
for a sufficiently small constant
c
, we have that
|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|\cdot s\leq c\cdot\sqrt{d}
and
2|\beta_{2}-\beta_{1}|\cdot\sqrt{s}\cdot|z_{1}|\leq 4\cdot\sqrt{s\cdot|\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2}|}\leq 4\sqrt{c\sqrt{d}}.
So, if
|z_{1}|\leq 2
and
c
is sufficiently small,
|\beta_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}^{2}|\cdot s+2|\beta_{2}-\beta_{1}|\cdot\sqrt{s}\cdot|z_{1}|\leq 0.001\sqrt{d-1}\leq 0.001\sqrt{d-1},
and
T\leq 0.1
. Since
T\leq 1
with probability
1
, and
|z_{1}|\leq 2
with at least
0.9
probability, we have
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}_{z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{T}\right.}{T}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{T}\right]}\leq 0.9\cdot 0.1+0.1\cdot 1\leq 0.5
. Overall, this means as long as
d\geq 2
and
s\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}
for sufficiently small
c
,
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1}^{2},Z_{2}^{2})\leq 0.5
.

In summary, if
s\leq\frac{0.01}{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}},
or
d\geq 2
and
s\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}
for sufficiently small
c
,
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{1}^{2},Z_{2}^{2})\leq 0.5
. Note that if
d=1
, and
c\leq 0.01
, then
s\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}\leq\frac{0.01}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}\leq\frac{0.01}{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}}
. Hence, it suffices for
s\leq\max\left(\frac{c}{(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}},\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}^{2}|}\right)
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main lower bound.

Proof of
1.9
.

First, we may assume that
\rho\leq 0.001
, as otherwise the lower bound equals
\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)
, which is required even for non-replicable testers.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{3}(\|\bar{X}\|;r)
be the weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm on
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
, following
Lemma

16
, and let
q_{3}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to[0,1]
represent the function where
\mathcal{A}_{3}\left(X;r\right)=1
if
r\leq q_{3}\left(\left\|\frac{X_{1}+\cdots+X_{s}}{s}\right\|\right)
and
0
otherwise.

First, suppose that
s\leq\frac{c}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}}
, where we recall that
c
is a sufficiently small constant. Let
t=\lfloor\frac{1}{300\rho}\rfloor
, and define
\beta_{i}=\alpha\cdot\frac{i}{t}
for each
i=0,1,\dots,t.
By
Lemma

17
, if
Z_{i}=\|\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\frac{I}{s})\|
where
\|\mu_{i}\|=\beta_{i}
, then
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{i},Z_{i+1})\leq 0.5
for all
0\leq i\leq t-1
, since
s\leq\frac{c}{(\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1})^{2}}
. Hence, by
Lemma

5
, as
\mathcal{A}_{3}
is a weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm, it cannot distinguish between
Z_{0}
and
Z_{t}
, and therefore cannot distinguish between
s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and
s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
with
\|\mu\|=\|\mu_{t}\|=\alpha
.

Alternatively, suppose that
s\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}\rho}
. Again, let
t=\lfloor\frac{1}{300\rho}\rfloor
, and this time define
\beta_{i}=\alpha\cdot\sqrt{\frac{i}{t}}
for each
i=0,1,\dots,t.
By
Lemma

17
, if
Z_{i}=\|\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\frac{I}{s})\|
where
\|\mu_{i}\|=\beta_{i}
, then
d_{\textit{TV}}(Z_{i},Z_{i+1})\leq 0.5
for all
0\leq i\leq t-1
, since
s\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{|\beta_{i}^{2}-\beta_{i+1}^{2}|}
. Hence, by
Lemma

5
, a weakly
\rho
-replicable algorithm cannot distinguish between
Z_{0}
and
Z_{t}
with
s
samples, and therefore cannot distinguish between
s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and
s
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I)
with
\|\mu\|=\|\mu_{t}\|=\alpha
.
∎

8
Replicable Hypothesis Selection via Testing

In hypothesis selection there is a known collection of distributions
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\dots,H_{n}\}
all over the same domain. Given samples from an unknown distribution
P
, our goal is to output an
i\in[n]
such that

\displaystyle d_{TV}(H_{i},P)\leq C\cdot\min_{H\in\mathcal{H}}d_{TV}(H,P)+\varepsilon.

(18)

for some constant
C
and desired small
\varepsilon
. Without replicability, the sample complexity of this problem is well-studied. Using
O(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}})
samples, we can achieve the above guarantee with
C=3

[
DL01
]
. Moreover, it is known that obtaining the guarantee with any constant
C<3
requires polynomially many samples in the domain size
[
BKM19
]
. In particular if the domain is infinite, we cannot get any finite sample complexity. Our main result in this section is a replicable algorithm for hypothesis selection.

Using our improved replicable coin-testing algorithm from
Section

6.3.1
as a key subroutine, we obtain the following sample complexity bound on replicable hypothesis selection. The challenge in hypothesis-selection is that we have a huge space of potential outputs (all
n
hypothesis), but we want to be stable in our outputs (for replicability). Our idea to get around this issue is to view hypothesis selection as an (adaptive) sequence of coin-testing problems, by partitioning the hypothesis in a binary-tree fashion. At each node in the tree, we have to pick if we want to descend down to the left or right branch which corresponds to one instance of the coin testing problem. The initial node contains all the
n
hypothesis and the final leaf nodes only contain one hypothesis.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
0\leq\varepsilon,\rho\leq 1
. There exists a
\rho
-replicable algorithm for hypothesis selection with optimal multiplicative approximation
C=3
which succeeds with high probability in
n
and takes samples

O\left\lparen\frac{\log^{5}n}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen

in the worst-case, and

O\left\lparen\frac{\log^{5}n}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen

in expectation.

Remark 5
.

An interesting question is if the sample complexity of Theorem
8.1
can be improved. We briefly note that this sample complexity cannot be improved by more than a
\log(n)^{3}\log(1/\varepsilon)
factor. This follows from Corollary 1.6 in
[
HIK+24
]
where it is shown that replicable mean estimation for a unit covariance Gaussian in
d
dimensions (up to additive error
\varepsilon
) requires
\Theta(\frac{d^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}})
samples. One way to solve this mean estimation problem is to discretize the unit sphere and solve hypothesis selection (any
C=O(1)
factor suffices in (
18
)) among
n=(1/\varepsilon)^{O(d)}
different possible hypothesis. Since this is a special case of the general hypothesis selection problem,
\Omega(\frac{\log^{2}(n)}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)})
samples must be necessary.

Proof.

To prove the theorem, we first recall how the algorithm from
[
DL01
]
works. For distinct
i,j
, define
S_{ij}=\{x\in[d]\mid H_{i}(x)\leq H_{j}(x)\}
and the
semi-distance

w_{j}(H_{i})=|H_{i}(S_{ij})-P(S_{ij})|
. Also define
W_{i}=\max_{j\neq i}w_{j}(H_{i})
and
W=\min_{i}W_{i}
. One can show that for any
i
such that
W_{i}=W
, the hypothesis
H_{i}
satisfies
Equation

18
with
C=3
and
\varepsilon=0
. Moreover, if
W_{i}\leq W+\varepsilon
, then
H_{i}
satisfies
Equation

18
with
C=3
and additive error
\varepsilon
.
Now using
m=O(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}})
samples, the algorithm from
[
DL01
]
provides an estimate
\hat{w}_{j}(i)
of
w_{j}(i)
satisfying that
|\hat{w}_{j}(i)-w_{j}(i)|\leq\varepsilon
for all pairs of distinct
i,j
with high probability in
n
. For each hypothesis
H_{i}
they then compute the quantity
\hat{W}_{i}=\max_{j\neq i}\hat{w}_{j}(H_{i})
and their algorithm returns an index
i
such that
\hat{W}_{i}
is minimal. Since all estimated semi-distances are within
\varepsilon
of the true semi-distance, it follows that
W_{i}\leq W+\varepsilon
, and they thus obtain the desired approximation guarantee.

For our replicable algorithm, we assume for simplicity that
n
is a power of two. Let us define
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon/\lg n
and
\rho_{0}=\rho/\lg n
and further for integers
0\leq j\leq\lg n
and
0\leq i<2^{j}
, we define

A_{i,j}=\{i2^{n-j}+k:1\leq k\leq 2^{n-j}\},

so that
|A_{i,j}|=n/2^{j}
and
A_{i,j}=A_{2i,j+1}\cup A_{2i+1,j+1}
for
0\leq j<\lg n
. Further define
\mathcal{H}_{i,j}:=\{H_{k}:k\in A_{i,j}\}
.
Finally, set
i_{0}=0
.

For
1\leq j\leq\lg n
, our algorithm iteratively computes an index
i_{j}
such that
0\leq i_{j}<2^{j}
and with high probability in
n
, the set
\mathcal{H}_{i_{j},j}
contains a hypothesis
H_{i}
with
W_{i}\leq W+j\varepsilon_{0}
. Since
|\mathcal{H}_{i_{\lg n},\lg n}|=1
, with high probability, the single hypothesis
H_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}_{i_{\lg n},\lg n}
has
W_{i}\leq W+\varepsilon
and returning this hypothesis, the desired approximation guarantee follows.

To construct
i_{j}
from
i_{j-1}
, we will reduce the subproblem to
\rho_{0}
-replicable coin testing.
We will consider the outcome of running the algorithm from
[
DL01
]
as a single sample from the coin.
In a single of these runs, when computing the estimate
\hat{W}_{i}
for a given hypothesis
H_{i}
, we do so with respect to the full set of hypotheses
\mathcal{H}
by taking
\hat{W}_{i}=\max_{j\in[n]}\hat{w}_{j}(H_{i})
. Denote by
p
the probability that in a single run, we return a hypothesis in
\mathcal{H}_{2i_{j},j+1}
. The probability of returning a hypothesis in
\mathcal{H}_{2i_{j}+1,j+1}
is thus
1-p
.
We will run our algorithm from
Theorem

6.6
on the resulting coin testing problem with
p_{0}=1/2
,
q_{0}=3/4
,
\rho_{0}
-replicability, and failure probability
\delta=\poly(1/n)
.
As replicable coin testing is technically defined for testing
p=p_{0}
or
p\geq q_{0}
, we will duplicate this process twice flipping the semantic meaning of heads so that the algorithm is correct with high probability when
p\leq 1/4
or
p\geq 3/4
.
To analyze our final algorithm, we need to argue about sample complexity, approximation guarantee, and replicability.

Sample Complexity:

Let
s_{j}
be the sample complexity of the coin testing algorithm at level
j
.
By
Theorem

6.6
,
s_{j}
is upper bounded in expectation by
\mathrm{\mathbf{E}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{s_{j}}\right.}{s_{j}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{s_{j}}\right]}=O\left\lparen\log n+\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\rho}\right\rparen
.
For each
j=1,\dots,\lg n
, the algorithm computes
s_{j}
maximum semi-distance estimators each based on
O\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right\rparen
samples. Thus the total number of samples is
O\left\lparen\frac{\log^{5}n}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho}\right\rparen
in expectation.

Via
Proposition

3
by stopping early and running the
O\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen
sample size non-replicable algorithm to ensure correctness, the sample complexity becomes
O\left\lparen\frac{\log^{5}n}{\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{2}}\right\rparen
in the worst-case.

Approximation:

We prove inductively that
\mathcal{H}_{i_{j},j}
contains a hypothesis
H_{i}
with
W_{i}\leq W+j\varepsilon_{0}
. This is trivially true for
j=0
, so suppose inductively that it holds for some
j
, and let us show that with high probability it also holds for
j+1
.
Suppose first that
1/4\leq p\leq 3/4
. With high probability, the algorithm from
[
DL01
]
returns a hypothesis
H_{i}
with

W_{i}\leq\min_{j\in A_{i_{j},j}}W_{j}+\varepsilon_{0}\leq W+\varepsilon_{0}(j+1),

where the last step uses the inductive hypothesis. In particular, in the case
1/4\leq p\leq 3/4
, then both of
\mathcal{H}_{2i_{j},j+1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2i_{j}+1,j+1}
must contain a hypothesis
H_{i}
with
W_{i}\leq W+(j+1)\varepsilon_{0}
and we are thus happy regardless of whether
i_{j+1}=2i_{j}
or
i_{j+1}=2i_{j}+1
. Now assume that
p<1/4
(the case
p>3/4
is similar). By the guarantee of replicable coin testing (
Theorem

6.6
), with high probability in
n
,
i_{j+1}=2i_{j}+1
, and since
p<1/4
,
\mathcal{H}_{2i_{j}+1,j+1}
does indeed contain a hypothesis
H_{i}
with
W_{i}\leq\min_{j\in A_{i_{j},j}}W(H_{j})
. The claim then again follows from the inductive hypothesis.

Union bounding over
j=0,\dots,\lg n-1
, we obtain that with high probability in
n
, the single hypothesis
H_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}_{i_{\lg n},\lg n}
has
W_{i}\leq W+\varepsilon
and the desired result follows.

Replicability:

Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be independent sets of samples, and let
\mathcal{A}
be our algorithm. Let
i_{0}^{(1)},\dots,i_{\lg n}^{(1)}
and
i_{0}^{(2)},\dots,i_{\lg n}^{(2)}
be the indices computes by
\mathcal{A}
when run on samples
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
respectively. To bound
\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r}\right.}{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r}\right]}\neq\mathcal{A}(X_{2},r))
, we bound the probability that there exists a
j
such that
i_{j}^{(1)}\neq i_{j}^{(2)}
. If no such
j
exists, then
\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r)=\mathcal{A}(X_{2},r)
. Denote by
E_{j}
the event that
i_{j}^{(1)}\neq i_{j}^{(2)}
. By the independence of the samples, and the
\rho_{0}
-replicability guaranteed by
Theorem

6.6
, it follows that

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{E_{j}\mid\bigcup_{k<j}E_{k}^{c}}\right.}{E_{j}\mid\bigcup_{k<j}E_{k}^{c}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{E_{j}\mid\bigcup_{k<j}E_{k}^{c}}\right]}\leq\rho_{0}.

Thus,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r)=\mathcal{A}(X_{2},r)}\right.}{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r)=\mathcal{A}(X_{2},r)}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\mathcal{A}(X_{1},r)=\mathcal{A}(X_{2},r)}\right]}\leq\mathrm{\mathbf{Pr}}\boldsymbol{\left[\vphantom{\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq\lg n}E_{j}}\right.}{\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq\lg n}E_{j}}\boldsymbol{\left.\vphantom{\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq\lg n}E_{j}}\right]}\leq 1-(1-\rho_{0})^{\lg n}\leq\rho,

as desired.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of
Proposition

4

Proof.

First, we check the accuracy guarantees. If
q(\mathcal{D})\geq 5T
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,
t:=h(X)\geq 5T\cdot(1-\delta)\geq 3T,
so the algorithm rejects since
\alpha=\frac{3T-h(X)}{T}\leq 0
. Otherwise, if
q(\mathcal{D})\leq T
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,
t:=h(X)\leq q+\rho\cdot\max(|q|,T)\leq 2T
, so the algorithm accepts since
\alpha=\frac{3T-h(X)}{T}\geq 1
.

Next, we check that
\mathcal{A}_{h,T}
is replicable. Assume
\rho\leq 1/10
, as otherwise the claim is trivial. Suppose that we sample
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}
i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}
. With probability at least
1-2\delta
, both
h(X)
and
h(X^{\prime})
are within
\rho\cdot\max(q,T)
from
q=q(\mathcal{D})
. If
q\geq 5T
, then with probability at least
1-2\delta
, both
t:=h(X)
and
t^{\prime}:=h(X^{\prime})
are at least
3T
, in which case the algorithm always rejects. If
q\leq 0
, then probability at least
1-2\delta
, both
t:=h(X)
and
t^{\prime}:=h(X^{\prime})
are at most
\rho\cdot T\leq T
, so the algorithm always accepts. Alternatively, with probability at least
1-2\delta
, both
t:=h(X)
and
t^{\prime}:=h(X^{\prime})
are within
5\rho\cdot T
of
q
, so are within
10\rho\cdot T
of each other. This means that
\alpha=\frac{3T-h(X)}{T}
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{3T-h(X^{\prime})}{T}
are within
10\rho
of each other. In this case, the probability of selecting
r\sim\Unif([0,1])
that lies between
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
is at most
10\rho
. Overall, there is at most a
10\rho+2\delta\leq 12\rho
failure probability that, over the random seed
r
and samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
,
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{A}_{h,T}
outputs a different result on
X
and
X^{\prime}
.
∎
